HVAC & Refrigeration English

Clarify comfort reports, equipment identity, airflow and capacity information, refrigeration records, scope changes, service access, and commissioning handovers.

For Heating, ventilation, air-conditioning, and refrigeration technicians, supervised apprentices, service coordinators, and mechanical crew leads.

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Separate a thermostat setpoint from a measured room temperature while preserving the time and location of a comfort complaint.
  • Reconcile conflicting equipment references using existing records and preserve an unverified relationship between components.
  • Translate supply and return labels into plain language while preserving the difference between a comfort report and an airflow measurement.
  • Compare like quantities, request missing equipment data, and distinguish refrigerant identification from approval to select or service a system.
  • Reconcile a historical report with a later observation and avoid treating an earlier maintenance concern as a proven cause.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

The projects, products, values, and records are fictional. This book teaches English, not equipment operation, diagnosis, installation, refrigerant handling, food-safety decisions, or compliance. Follow actual training, authorization, manufacturer instructions, site procedures, and applicable requirements. No dialogue authorizes roof access, testing, adjustment, refrigerant work, food release, or a system alteration. Equipment readings and document receipt do not establish safety or overall performance.

01Clarifying comfort complaints and thermostat readingsTwenty-two is the target, not the finding+

Your goal

Separate a thermostat setpoint from a measured room temperature while preserving the time and location of a comfort complaint.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Twenty-two is the target, not the finding

Office manager Asha tells service coordinator Ben that the meeting room is 22 degrees Celsius. The number is actually the thermostat setpoint, not a confirmed ambient-temperature reading. Staff report feeling warm there after 14:00. No cause or equipment fault has been established. Ben needs to clarify the value, keep the staff report attributed, and record the afternoon pattern. The conversation uses existing information and gives no instruction to adjust or test the equipment.

Asha: Office manager / Ben: Service coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does 22 degrees Celsius represent?

Answer and explanation

B. The thermostat setpoint

The brief identifies the number as the target setting rather than a measured room condition.

2. When do staff report feeling warm?

Answer and explanation

C. After 14:00

The supplied pattern is an afternoon report beginning after 14:00.

3. What is established about the equipment?

Answer and explanation

B. No cause or fault has been established

The complaint and setpoint do not establish a cause or equipment condition.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Twenty-two degrees Celsius is the setpoint in this case. It must not be relabeled as ambient temperature. The complaint concerns staff feeling warm in the meeting room after 14:00, not a verified temperature or fault.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
HVAC
Heating, ventilation, and air conditioning.discuss the HVAC system
thermostat
Control associated with maintaining a selected temperature.identify the thermostat
setpoint
Target value used by a control system.distinguish the setpoint
ambient temperature
Temperature of the surrounding air at the relevant location.record ambient temperature
temperature reading
Value reported by a temperature-measuring device.identify the temperature reading
degrees Celsius
Temperature unit used in the supplied report.state the value in degrees Celsius
comfort complaint
Occupant report that environmental conditions feel uncomfortable.log a comfort complaint
occupant feedback
Account supplied by people using the space.preserve occupant feedback
sensible cooling
Heat removal associated with lowering air temperature without counting moisture condensation.distinguish sensible cooling
afternoon pattern
Reported tendency occurring later in the day.record the afternoon pattern
sensor
Device responding to a physical condition for measurement or control.identify the relevant sensor
measured value
Quantity established by a measurement.distinguish the measured value
displayed value
Number shown by a device or interface.clarify the displayed value
target temperature
Temperature the control is intended to seek.state the target temperature
latent cooling
Heat removal associated here with condensing water vapor from air.account for latent cooling
relative humidity
Water-vapor level relative to saturation at the same temperature.report relative humidity
occupancy
Use of a space by people.describe room occupancy
control schedule
Timing arrangement for control settings or operation.review the control schedule
zone
Defined area treated as a control or service region.identify the relevant zone
heat gain
Heat added to a space from its surroundings or internal sources.assess heat gain
equipment fault
Established malfunction of a system component.avoid assuming an equipment fault
reported period
Time span described in an account.preserve the reported period
service description
Concise account used to request relevant attention.clarify the service description
attributed report
Statement linked to the person or group who supplied it.retain an attributed report

A comfort report is useful even without a confirmed ambient reading. Retain the people, place, and afternoon pattern. Do not diagnose a sensor failure, prescribe a setting change, or claim normal performance from the target alone.

C Notice the language

Correct the number label without dismissing the people

A setpoint can be accurate while a comfort complaint is still meaningful. Ask what the displayed value represents before treating it as a measurement. Acknowledge the occupants and preserve their reported pattern without diagnosing a fault or prescribing a control change.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask what the number means
Is 22 the setpoint or a measured room temperature?
Name the target
The thermostat is set to 22 degrees Celsius.
Preserve the missing reading
No confirmed ambient reading has been supplied.
Acknowledge the people
Staff report feeling warm in the meeting room.
Keep the time pattern
The reports concern the period after 14:00.
Avoid a contradiction
A target setting does not disprove a comfort complaint.
Separate the evidence
We have a setpoint and an occupant report, not a diagnosis.
Avoid assigning a failed part
No sensor fault has been established.
Ask about existing information
What does the current record identify that value as?
Keep the location
Please keep meeting room in the service description.
Use the correct unit
The value is stated in degrees Celsius.
Avoid prescribing an adjustment
This clarification does not specify a new setting.
Retain the account
I will record what the staff have reported.
Avoid inventing a measured excess
We do not have a measured temperature above the target.
Read back the distinction
Setpoint 22; staff feel warm after 14:00; ambient reading unavailable.
Close with an accurate request
I will pass on that description for the relevant assessment.
6 language and precision notes
Set to
Describes a target, not necessarily a measured condition.
Report feeling
Attributes a subjective experience without dismissing it.
After 14:00
Preserves the reported period without inventing an exact onset.
Not supplied
Means information is absent from the account, not that measurement is impossible.
Does not disprove
Allows the setpoint and occupant experience to coexist.
No fault established
Avoids both a diagnosis and an unsupported fault-free assurance.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which wording accurately uses the number?

Answer and explanation

A. The thermostat setpoint is 22 degrees Celsius

The value identifies a target setting, not any of the proposed measurements.

2. Which summary preserves the complaint?

Answer and explanation

B. Staff feel warm in the meeting room after 14:00; ambient reading not supplied

The summary keeps attribution, place, period, and missing measurement intact.

3. What should Ben avoid inferring?

Answer and explanation

C. A particular equipment fault

No diagnostic evidence establishes a component failure in the supplied facts.

4. Which question best clarifies the displayed value?

Answer and explanation

A. Does the record label 22 as the target or the measured room temperature?

The bounded question identifies the value using existing information without assuming a fault.

E Conversations

Twenty-two is the target, not the finding

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Asha: Office manager / Ben: Service coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • afternoon pattern
  • ambient temperature
  • equipment fault
  • degrees Celsius
  • target temperature
  • attributed report
  • service description
  • occupant feedback
  • setpoint
  • temperature reading
  1. Asha
    Ben, the meeting room is twenty-two degrees, but staff keep saying it feels warm after lunch. How should I describe that without sounding contradictory?
  2. Ben
    Before I record it, is twenty-two the 1, or is it a separate measured room-temperature value? Those mean different things in a service request.
  3. Asha
    It's the thermostat setting, not a separate room measurement. I copied the number from the information the office team gave me.
  4. Ben
    Then we should not call it the 2. We can record the target and the staff account without treating the setting as proof of the room condition.
  5. Asha
    The reports concern the meeting room after two in the afternoon. I don't have the same complaint about every room or every part of the day.
  6. Ben
    That is a useful 3. I will retain the meeting-room location and after 14:00, so the request does not become a whole-office complaint.
  7. Asha
    Will the service team dismiss the complaint if the request says the thermostat is set to twenty-two? The people still feel uncomfortable.
  8. Ben
    No. We can preserve the 4 and clarify the number at the same time. A target does not establish what everyone in the room actually experiences.
  9. Asha
    I had written possible sensor failure to make the request more specific. But the information doesn't establish a failed sensor, does it?
  10. Ben
    It is. No 5 has been established. We should not name a failed sensor or another component simply to give the complaint a technical-sounding cause.
  11. Asha
    Please include Celsius. Our overseas colleagues sometimes use Fahrenheit, and I'd rather the unit didn't disappear when the request is forwarded.
  12. Ben
    Certainly. I will state 6 explicitly. A number without its unit and its meaning can become misleading when the message is forwarded.
  13. Asha
    When we say no confirmed reading supplied, we mean this account lacks it. We don't know whether someone has measured the room on another occasion.
  14. Ben
    Yes. The missing 7 is a limit of this information. It does not establish the history of every measurement or the condition of the equipment.
  15. Asha
    I'll keep the request to the existing information. I don't want my summary mistaken for an instruction to change the thermostat or test a component.
  16. Ben
    Agreed. We are clarifying a 8, not issuing control instructions. Any assessment belongs with the relevant team and its actual procedures.
  17. Asha
    Could you read it back with the target and missing measurement separate? I need a short version for the facilities manager.
  18. Ben
    Here is the 9: staff feel warm in the meeting room after 14:00; thermostat setpoint 22 degrees Celsius; no confirmed ambient reading supplied; cause unestablished.
  19. Asha
    That preserves the staff account without inventing a measured temperature above target. I'll stop describing twenty-two as the actual room condition.
  20. Ben
    I will pass on that record. It distinguishes the 10 from the actual condition that still needs assessment, while keeping the staff's reported experience visible.

Answers and explanations
  1. setpoint Turn 2. Setpoint identifies the target setting, which must not be mistaken for a room measurement.
  2. ambient temperature Turn 4. Ambient temperature is the surrounding-air condition, for which no confirmed reading is supplied.
  3. afternoon pattern Turn 6. Afternoon pattern preserves the reported period after 14:00 without inventing continuous discomfort or exact onset.
  4. occupant feedback Turn 8. Occupant feedback records the staff experience alongside the target rather than treating one as a contradiction.
  5. equipment fault Turn 10. Equipment fault has not been established by the setpoint or the comfort report.
  6. degrees Celsius Turn 12. Degrees Celsius supplies the unit attached to the stated target value.
  7. temperature reading Turn 14. Temperature reading is absent from this account; that does not assert that no measurement ever exists.
  8. service description Turn 16. Service description communicates the known complaint without prescribing tests or a control adjustment.
  9. attributed report Turn 18. Attributed report links the comfort account to staff and keeps the target and missing reading separate.
  10. target temperature Turn 20. Target temperature is the intended control value, not proof of actual room conditions or performance.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Asha: Ben, the meeting room is twenty-two degrees, but staff keep saying it feels warm after lunch. How should I describe that without sounding contradictory?
  2. Ben: Before I record it, is twenty-two the setpoint, or is it a separate measured room-temperature value? Those mean different things in a service request.
  3. Asha: It's the thermostat setting, not a separate room measurement. I copied the number from the information the office team gave me.
  4. Ben: Then we should not call it the ambient temperature. We can record the target and the staff account without treating the setting as proof of the room condition.
  5. Asha: The reports concern the meeting room after two in the afternoon. I don't have the same complaint about every room or every part of the day.
  6. Ben: That is a useful afternoon pattern. I will retain the meeting-room location and after 14:00, so the request does not become a whole-office complaint.
  7. Asha: Will the service team dismiss the complaint if the request says the thermostat is set to twenty-two? The people still feel uncomfortable.
  8. Ben: No. We can preserve the occupant feedback and clarify the number at the same time. A target does not establish what everyone in the room actually experiences.
  9. Asha: I had written possible sensor failure to make the request more specific. But the information doesn't establish a failed sensor, does it?
  10. Ben: It is. No equipment fault has been established. We should not name a failed sensor or another component simply to give the complaint a technical-sounding cause.
  11. Asha: Please include Celsius. Our overseas colleagues sometimes use Fahrenheit, and I'd rather the unit didn't disappear when the request is forwarded.
  12. Ben: Certainly. I will state degrees Celsius explicitly. A number without its unit and its meaning can become misleading when the message is forwarded.
  13. Asha: When we say no confirmed reading supplied, we mean this account lacks it. We don't know whether someone has measured the room on another occasion.
  14. Ben: Yes. The missing temperature reading is a limit of this information. It does not establish the history of every measurement or the condition of the equipment.
  15. Asha: I'll keep the request to the existing information. I don't want my summary mistaken for an instruction to change the thermostat or test a component.
  16. Ben: Agreed. We are clarifying a service description, not issuing control instructions. Any assessment belongs with the relevant team and its actual procedures.
  17. Asha: Could you read it back with the target and missing measurement separate? I need a short version for the facilities manager.
  18. Ben: Here is the attributed report: staff feel warm in the meeting room after 14:00; thermostat setpoint 22 degrees Celsius; no confirmed ambient reading supplied; cause unestablished.
  19. Asha: That preserves the staff account without inventing a measured temperature above target. I'll stop describing twenty-two as the actual room condition.
  20. Ben: I will pass on that record. It distinguishes the target temperature from the actual condition that still needs assessment, while keeping the staff's reported experience visible.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Twenty-two is the target

A technician clarifies the fictional meeting-room complaint with the office manager.

  1. Technician: Your request says the room is twenty-two degrees. Which display value is that?
  2. Office manager: The thermostat says target twenty-two Celsius. Staff still feel warm after two.
  3. Technician: Then twenty-two is the setpoint, not a confirmed ambient-temperature reading in the room.
  4. Office manager: I hadn't distinguished those. Does that make the staff complaint less useful?
  5. Technician: No, its location and timing are useful. We'll record the meeting-room thermal zone.
  6. Office manager: Should I report a failed cooling system because the target isn't what people feel?
  7. Technician: The complaint doesn't establish that cause. We need the relevant assessment and measurements.
  8. Office manager: I'll describe feeling warm after two, with the target clearly labeled as a setpoint.
  9. Technician: Please include whether that pattern comes from your observation or reports from colleagues.
  10. Office manager: It's from the afternoon meeting team. I'll attribute it to them in the request.
Warm at one desk, not the whole floor

A technician checks a comfort report with a facilities contact.

  1. Technician: The request says the floor is too warm. Which occupied area prompted it?
  2. Facilities contact: Three staff near the west windows reported discomfort during yesterday's afternoon meeting period.
  3. Technician: Have other areas reported the same concern, or is that not yet known?
  4. Facilities contact: We haven't gathered other reports. I shouldn't describe the whole floor as affected.
  5. Technician: Let's identify those desks and the time without assuming the same thermal zone everywhere.
  6. Facilities contact: I can provide the seating plan, but I don't know the actual zone boundaries.
  7. Technician: That's useful background. The system records can establish how the areas are grouped.
  8. Facilities contact: Should I move people around to test whether the complaint follows their seats?
  9. Technician: No experiment is needed for this conversation. Please provide their existing accounts only.
  10. Facilities contact: I'll send those accounts and the plan, keeping the wider-floor condition unconfirmed.
The guest says stuffy

A technician clarifies a hotel's comfort wording with its duty manager.

  1. Technician: The guest described the room as stuffy. What did they mean by that?
  2. Duty manager: They mentioned feeling warm and a noticeable odor, not a specific temperature reading.
  3. Technician: Those are distinct reports. Let's retain both without translating stuffy into one technical diagnosis.
  4. Duty manager: The booking form only has a cooling-problem category. Is that too narrow?
  5. Technician: It can route the request, but the description should preserve the guest's actual concerns.
  6. Duty manager: I'll include the room, evening timing, warmth, and odor, with no confirmed cause.
  7. Technician: Please note whether the guest reported this once or on several evenings.
  8. Duty manager: They mentioned two evenings, but I don't have dates for both yet.
  9. Technician: Record that uncertainty rather than inventing dates; the history can be clarified at the visit.
  10. Duty manager: I'll arrange room access and keep the report separate from any proposed remedy.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Twenty-two is the target, not the finding
  1. Read turns 1-10. Stress setpoint, no ambient reading, meeting room, and after two to preserve the complaint's meaning.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep Celsius with the target and distinguish missing information from no measurement ever taken.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Label the value correctly

Complete the service report using the target, time pattern, and missing measurement. Do not add a diagnosis.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: "The thermostat ___ is 22 degrees Celsius."

Answer and explanation

B. setpoint

Setpoint correctly identifies the target rather than a measured ambient temperature.

2. Coordinator: "Staff feel warm after ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 14:00

The time preserves the supplied afternoon complaint without inventing another period.

3. Manager: "No confirmed ___ reading is supplied."

Answer and explanation

A. ambient

Ambient identifies the actual surrounding-air measurement that is missing from the account.

4. Coordinator: "The cause remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unestablished

Neither the setpoint nor the report establishes a technical cause.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Clarifying comfort complaints and thermostat readings
Original fictional case: Office manager Asha tells service coordinator Ben that the meeting room is 22 degrees Celsius. The number is actually the thermostat setpoint, not a confirmed ambient-temperature reading. Staff report feeling warm there after 14:00. No cause or equipment fault has been established. Ben needs to clarify the value, keep the staff report attributed, and record the afternoon pattern. The conversation uses existing information and gives no instruction to adjust or test the equipment.
Language workshop: Twenty-two is the target, not the finding
Communication goal: Separate a thermostat setpoint from a measured room temperature while preserving the time and location of a comfort complaint.
Language guidance: Twenty-two degrees Celsius is the setpoint in this case. It must not be relabeled as ambient temperature. The complaint concerns staff feeling warm in the meeting room after 14:00, not a verified temperature or fault.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Is 22 the setpoint or a measured room temperature? / The thermostat is set to 22 degrees Celsius. / No confirmed ambient reading has been supplied. / Staff report feeling warm in the meeting room. / The reports concern the period after 14:00. / A target setting does not disprove a comfort complaint. / We have a setpoint and an occupant report, not a diagnosis. / No sensor fault has been established. / What does the current record identify that value as? / Please keep meeting room in the service description. / The value is stated in degrees Celsius. / This clarification does not specify a new setting. / I will record what the staff have reported. / We do not have a measured temperature above the target. / Setpoint 22; staff feel warm after 14:00; ambient reading unavailable. / I will pass on that description for the relevant assessment.
Vocabulary: HVAC: Heating, ventilation, and air conditioning. / thermostat: Control associated with maintaining a selected temperature. / setpoint: Target value used by a control system. / ambient temperature: Temperature of the surrounding air at the relevant location. / temperature reading: Value reported by a temperature-measuring device. / degrees Celsius: Temperature unit used in the supplied report. / comfort complaint: Occupant report that environmental conditions feel uncomfortable. / occupant feedback: Account supplied by people using the space. / sensible cooling: Heat removal associated with lowering air temperature without counting moisture condensation. / afternoon pattern: Reported tendency occurring later in the day. / sensor: Device responding to a physical condition for measurement or control. / measured value: Quantity established by a measurement. / displayed value: Number shown by a device or interface. / target temperature: Temperature the control is intended to seek. / latent cooling: Heat removal associated here with condensing water vapor from air. / relative humidity: Water-vapor level relative to saturation at the same temperature. / occupancy: Use of a space by people. / control schedule: Timing arrangement for control settings or operation. / zone: Defined area treated as a control or service region. / heat gain: Heat added to a space from its surroundings or internal sources. / equipment fault: Established malfunction of a system component. / reported period: Time span described in an account. / service description: Concise account used to request relevant attention. / attributed report: Statement linked to the person or group who supplied it.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress setpoint, no ambient reading, meeting room, and after two to preserve the complaint's meaning. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep Celsius with the target and distinguish missing information from no measurement ever taken. Complete and check the service exchange. Read the actual report without inventing a sensor fault or setting change.
Partner: Asha: Office manager / Ben: Service coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the service report using the target, time pattern, and missing measurement. Do not add a diagnosis.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Asha: Could you read it back with the target and missing measurement separate? I need a short version for the facilities manager.
Ben: Here is the attributed report: staff feel warm in the meeting room after 14:00; thermostat setpoint 22 degrees Celsius; no confirmed ambient reading supplied; cause unestablished.
Asha: That preserves the staff account without inventing a measured temperature above target. I'll stop describing twenty-two as the actual room condition.
Ben: I will pass on that record. It distinguishes the target temperature from the actual condition that still needs assessment, while keeping the staff's reported experience visible.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Clarifying comfort complaints and thermostat readings
Original fictional case: Office manager Asha tells service coordinator Ben that the meeting room is 22 degrees Celsius. The number is actually the thermostat setpoint, not a confirmed ambient-temperature reading. Staff report feeling warm there after 14:00. No cause or equipment fault has been established. Ben needs to clarify the value, keep the staff report attributed, and record the afternoon pattern. The conversation uses existing information and gives no instruction to adjust or test the equipment.
Language workshop: Twenty-two is the target, not the finding
Communication goal: Separate a thermostat setpoint from a measured room temperature while preserving the time and location of a comfort complaint.
Language guidance: Twenty-two degrees Celsius is the setpoint in this case. It must not be relabeled as ambient temperature. The complaint concerns staff feeling warm in the meeting room after 14:00, not a verified temperature or fault.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Is 22 the setpoint or a measured room temperature? / The thermostat is set to 22 degrees Celsius. / No confirmed ambient reading has been supplied. / Staff report feeling warm in the meeting room. / The reports concern the period after 14:00. / A target setting does not disprove a comfort complaint. / We have a setpoint and an occupant report, not a diagnosis. / No sensor fault has been established. / What does the current record identify that value as? / Please keep meeting room in the service description. / The value is stated in degrees Celsius. / This clarification does not specify a new setting. / I will record what the staff have reported. / We do not have a measured temperature above the target. / Setpoint 22; staff feel warm after 14:00; ambient reading unavailable. / I will pass on that description for the relevant assessment.
Vocabulary: HVAC: Heating, ventilation, and air conditioning. / thermostat: Control associated with maintaining a selected temperature. / setpoint: Target value used by a control system. / ambient temperature: Temperature of the surrounding air at the relevant location. / temperature reading: Value reported by a temperature-measuring device. / degrees Celsius: Temperature unit used in the supplied report. / comfort complaint: Occupant report that environmental conditions feel uncomfortable. / occupant feedback: Account supplied by people using the space. / sensible cooling: Heat removal associated with lowering air temperature without counting moisture condensation. / afternoon pattern: Reported tendency occurring later in the day. / sensor: Device responding to a physical condition for measurement or control. / measured value: Quantity established by a measurement. / displayed value: Number shown by a device or interface. / target temperature: Temperature the control is intended to seek. / latent cooling: Heat removal associated here with condensing water vapor from air. / relative humidity: Water-vapor level relative to saturation at the same temperature. / occupancy: Use of a space by people. / control schedule: Timing arrangement for control settings or operation. / zone: Defined area treated as a control or service region. / heat gain: Heat added to a space from its surroundings or internal sources. / equipment fault: Established malfunction of a system component. / reported period: Time span described in an account. / service description: Concise account used to request relevant attention. / attributed report: Statement linked to the person or group who supplied it.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress setpoint, no ambient reading, meeting room, and after two to preserve the complaint's meaning. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep Celsius with the target and distinguish missing information from no measurement ever taken. Complete and check the service exchange. Read the actual report without inventing a sensor fault or setting change.
Partner: Asha: Office manager / Ben: Service coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the service report using the target, time pattern, and missing measurement. Do not add a diagnosis.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Asha: Could you read it back with the target and missing measurement separate? I need a short version for the facilities manager.
Ben: Here is the attributed report: staff feel warm in the meeting room after 14:00; thermostat setpoint 22 degrees Celsius; no confirmed ambient reading supplied; cause unestablished.
Asha: That preserves the staff account without inventing a measured temperature above target. I'll stop describing twenty-two as the actual room condition.
Ben: I will pass on that record. It distinguishes the target temperature from the actual condition that still needs assessment, while keeping the staff's reported experience visible.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Identifying equipment and linked componentsAC-4, CU-4, and AH-4 are not yet a pairing+

Your goal

Reconcile conflicting equipment references using existing records and preserve an unverified relationship between components.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

AC-4, CU-4, and AH-4 are not yet a pairing

A school service request calls the system AC-4. The roof plan labels an outdoor unit CU-4, while the indoor equipment register identifies air handler AH-4. Their relationship is not confirmed. Facilities manager Mira has the equipment register and discusses the request with service coordinator Owen. They need to preserve the three source references and seek confirmation through the records process. Neither person needs to access the roof or inspect equipment to clarify the existing documents.

Mira: Facilities manager / Owen: Service coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which label appears in the service request?

Answer and explanation

C. AC-4

AC-4 is the request label, distinct from the plan and register references.

2. What does the register identify as AH-4?

Answer and explanation

A. An indoor air handler

The indoor equipment register explicitly identifies an air handler as AH-4.

3. What is unresolved?

Answer and explanation

D. The relationship between CU-4 and AH-4

The supplied documents do not confirm how the two components are related.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

AC-4 appears in the request, CU-4 on the roof plan, and AH-4 in the indoor register. Keep all three with their sources. A shared numeral does not confirm a component pairing or served area.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
equipment register
Record identifying equipment and relevant asset information.consult the equipment register
asset tag
Assigned identifier used to distinguish an equipment item.preserve the asset tag
system reference
Label used to identify an overall system.clarify the system reference
component reference
Label identifying a particular part of a system.retain the component reference
air handler
Equipment that moves and may condition air in a building system.identify the air handler
outdoor unit
Equipment assembly located outside the served interior.identify the outdoor unit
condensing unit
Assembly associated with refrigerant compression and heat rejection in relevant systems.verify the condensing-unit reference
indoor unit
Equipment assembly located within the building or served space.identify the indoor unit
equipment pairing
Confirmed relationship between components intended to operate together.verify the equipment pairing
served area
Space associated with the service from an identified system.confirm the served area
roof plan
Drawing showing relevant roof-level features and equipment.cite the roof plan
record source
Document or system from which information was obtained.identify the record source
cross-reference
Link connecting corresponding entries in different records.establish a cross-reference
label discrepancy
Difference between identifiers in related information.record the label discrepancy
model number
Manufacturer identifier for a product design or version.confirm the model number
serial number
Identifier distinguishing an individual manufactured unit.record the serial number
nameplate
Manufacturer label carrying equipment identification or ratings.use recorded nameplate information
asset hierarchy
Organized relationship between systems and their components.clarify the asset hierarchy
service request
Record asking for attention to an identified issue or system.reconcile the service request
records reconciliation
Process of resolving differences between documented information.complete records reconciliation
unverified link
Relationship that has not been confirmed.retain an unverified link
identification query
Request to clarify which equipment is meant.raise an identification query
record custodian
Person responsible for maintaining or providing a record.identify the record custodian
confirmed mapping
Verified correspondence between identifiers or components.obtain confirmed mapping

Mira already has the equipment register. The next step is records reconciliation through the responsible process, not asking someone to reach a roof or inspect a nameplate. Recorded information must still be verified in context.

C Notice the language

Treat a familiar label as a starting point

Site shorthand may name a whole system while formal documents identify separate components. Similar numbering can suggest a question, but it is not proof of connection. Cite each label with its source so the next person can reconcile the records without silently merging them.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Quote the request
The service request calls the system AC-4.
Quote the plan
The roof plan labels an outdoor unit CU-4.
Quote the register
The indoor register identifies air handler AH-4.
Preserve source attribution
Each label should stay attached to its source.
Avoid a number shortcut
The shared four does not prove that the components are paired.
Name the unresolved link
The relationship between CU-4 and AH-4 is unverified.
Use existing records
You already have the equipment register.
Request reconciliation
We need a confirmed cross-reference between these entries.
Separate system and component
A system label may not be the identifier of one physical unit.
Avoid assumed renaming
I will not replace AC-4 with CU-4 by guesswork.
Keep the served area open
We have not confirmed the served area from these labels.
Distinguish identifiers
A model number and a serial number identify different things.
Keep the request precise
Please retain all three references in the query.
Avoid directing access
This is a document query, not a request for roof access.
Read back the gap
The records name the items but do not confirm their relationship.
Close with the needed result
We need confirmed mapping before merging the references.
6 language and precision notes
Labels versus connects
A label names an item; it does not establish the item's relationship to another.
Appears in
Attributes an identifier to a particular source.
May not
Warns against assuming equivalence without declaring the records wrong.
By guesswork
Identifies an unsupported substitution of one label for another.
Already have
Directs attention to available information rather than new physical access.
Before merging
Makes verification a condition of consolidating records.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which summary is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Request AC-4; roof plan CU-4; register AH-4; relationship unverified

The summary preserves the separate source labels and unresolved relationship.

2. What should Owen ask Mira to provide?

Answer and explanation

D. The relevant existing register information for reconciliation

Existing source records support reconciliation; renaming, pairing by number, or borrowing an unrelated room would add unsupported information.

3. Why is the numeral four insufficient?

Answer and explanation

B. Similar numbering does not establish verified component relationships

An identifier pattern is not evidence that the recorded components form a confirmed pairing.

4. What should remain visible until clarified?

Answer and explanation

A. The source of each label and the unverified link

Keeping sources and uncertainty supports accurate reconciliation without losing original information.

E Conversations

AC-4, CU-4, and AH-4 are not yet a pairing

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mira: Facilities manager / Owen: Service coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • equipment register
  • equipment pairing
  • confirmed mapping
  • record source
  • model number
  • air handler
  • system reference
  • served area
  • cross-reference
  • service request
  1. Mira
    Owen, the school's request says AC-4, but I can't match that cleanly to the project documents. I don't want to send the wrong reference.
  2. Owen
    Let's keep the 1 beside each identifier. What does the roof plan call the outdoor equipment, and what does the indoor register say?
  3. Mira
    The roof plan labels the outdoor unit CU-4. The indoor register lists AH-4, an air handler. Only the service request uses AC-4.
  4. Owen
    So the 2 has its own reference. AC-4 may refer to the system more broadly; we shouldn't treat all three labels as one physical item.
  5. Mira
    They all end in four. I thought that would be enough to link the indoor and outdoor entries, but the records don't actually confirm it.
  6. Owen
    Then leave the 3 unverified. The shared numeral suggests a question to resolve; it doesn't establish that those components operate together.
  7. Mira
    I have the register here already. Can we resolve the question through the records process instead of asking someone to go onto the roof?
  8. Owen
    Yes. Send the relevant 4 entries and roof-plan reference. We're reconciling existing documents, not requesting physical access or a new equipment inspection.
  9. Mira
    Would it help if I changed AC-4 to CU-4 on the request now? At least that label would agree with the roof plan.
  10. Owen
    Please retain the original 5 wording. Add the plan and register references alongside it; changing the label prematurely could narrow what the requester meant.
  11. Mira
    Right, a request about the whole system might become one about just the outdoor unit. We need the relationship before choosing the final reference.
  12. Owen
    Exactly. A 6 and a component identifier can describe different levels of the equipment arrangement. The records need a supported link between them.
  13. Mira
    There may be model and serial fields in the register too. Those aren't interchangeable with the school's asset tags, are they?
  14. Owen
    No. A 7 identifies a product design or version; a serial number distinguishes a manufactured unit. Keep each field with its recorded equipment item.
  15. Mira
    Another request mentions the meeting room. Can I borrow that location to make this one more useful, or would that introduce an unsupported connection?
  16. Owen
    Don't borrow it. The 8 still needs confirmation for these references. A plausible location from a different request isn't evidence for this equipment relationship.
  17. Mira
    Please phrase the query without saying one document must be wrong. The labels may describe different things correctly, even if their connection is missing.
  18. Owen
    Ask for a confirmed 9 between request AC-4, plan item CU-4, and register item AH-4, including the component relationship and the served area.
  19. Mira
    I'll send all three sources intact. That should let the record owner investigate the actual mapping instead of guessing which label we preferred.
  20. Owen
    Once we receive 10, we can clarify the service reference. Until then, keep the separate labels and unresolved relationship visible in the request.

Answers and explanations
  1. record source Turn 2. Record source identifies where each label comes from instead of treating the labels as interchangeable.
  2. air handler Turn 4. Air handler is the indoor item named AH-4 in the register, not a confirmed synonym for CU-4.
  3. equipment pairing Turn 6. Equipment pairing is the relationship that remains unverified despite the shared numeral.
  4. equipment register Turn 8. Equipment register is already available to Mira and can support the document reconciliation.
  5. service request Turn 10. Service request retains the original AC-4 wording while its relationship to other labels is clarified.
  6. system reference Turn 12. System reference may identify the overall arrangement rather than one physical component.
  7. model number Turn 14. Model number identifies a product design or version, unlike the individual-unit serial number.
  8. served area Turn 16. Served area must be established for the identified system rather than borrowed from another request.
  9. cross-reference Turn 18. Cross-reference asks how the documented labels correspond without presuming that any one record is wrong.
  10. confirmed mapping Turn 20. Confirmed mapping supplies verified correspondence before labels are merged or the request is relabeled.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mira: Owen, the school's request says AC-4, but I can't match that cleanly to the project documents. I don't want to send the wrong reference.
  2. Owen: Let's keep the record source beside each identifier. What does the roof plan call the outdoor equipment, and what does the indoor register say?
  3. Mira: The roof plan labels the outdoor unit CU-4. The indoor register lists AH-4, an air handler. Only the service request uses AC-4.
  4. Owen: So the air handler has its own reference. AC-4 may refer to the system more broadly; we shouldn't treat all three labels as one physical item.
  5. Mira: They all end in four. I thought that would be enough to link the indoor and outdoor entries, but the records don't actually confirm it.
  6. Owen: Then leave the equipment pairing unverified. The shared numeral suggests a question to resolve; it doesn't establish that those components operate together.
  7. Mira: I have the register here already. Can we resolve the question through the records process instead of asking someone to go onto the roof?
  8. Owen: Yes. Send the relevant equipment register entries and roof-plan reference. We're reconciling existing documents, not requesting physical access or a new equipment inspection.
  9. Mira: Would it help if I changed AC-4 to CU-4 on the request now? At least that label would agree with the roof plan.
  10. Owen: Please retain the original service request wording. Add the plan and register references alongside it; changing the label prematurely could narrow what the requester meant.
  11. Mira: Right, a request about the whole system might become one about just the outdoor unit. We need the relationship before choosing the final reference.
  12. Owen: Exactly. A system reference and a component identifier can describe different levels of the equipment arrangement. The records need a supported link between them.
  13. Mira: There may be model and serial fields in the register too. Those aren't interchangeable with the school's asset tags, are they?
  14. Owen: No. A model number identifies a product design or version; a serial number distinguishes a manufactured unit. Keep each field with its recorded equipment item.

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Which number belongs to which unit?

A technician reviews the school's AC-4 request with the facilities manager.

  1. Technician: Your request names AC-4, but the register uses AH-4 and CU-4 separately.
  2. Facilities manager: AH-4 is the air handler. CU-4 is listed as the outdoor condensing unit.
  3. Technician: Does the register explicitly link them, or are you matching their final numbers?
  4. Facilities manager: I'm matching the numbers. I don't see a documented relationship on this page.
  5. Technician: Then let's keep the pairing unverified and request the relevant system record.
  6. Facilities manager: I can obtain that from the building archive without anyone visiting the roof.
  7. Technician: Good. We also need to attach the service history to the correct asset.
  8. Facilities manager: The previous contractor's invoice says unit four, which may be equally ambiguous.
  9. Technician: Please retain that wording and its source rather than assigning it to either component.
  10. Facilities manager: I'll request the detailed job record and give you the two confirmed tag entries.
The model repeats, the serial does not

A technician checks a parts enquiry with a service coordinator.

  1. Technician: Both units share the model number, but the parts enquiry identifies only one serial number.
  2. Service coordinator: I assumed the same model meant we could use either unit's service history.
  3. Technician: A model identifies a product type; the serial distinguishes the individual manufactured unit.
  4. Service coordinator: Then the earlier repair record might belong to the neighboring unit rather than this one.
  5. Technician: Exactly. Let's reconcile the asset tag and serial before applying that history to this enquiry.
  6. Service coordinator: The register includes both. I can provide their entries without changing any labels.
  7. Technician: Please do, and preserve any mismatch for review instead of selecting the most convenient number.
  8. Service coordinator: I'll identify which record came from the old invoice and which from the register.
  9. Technician: That will let the service lead assess the linkage using actual sources.
  10. Service coordinator: I'll keep the parts request pending until its intended equipment identity is clear.
The evaporator is not the whole system

A technician explains a refrigerator service record to a cafe manager.

  1. Technician: The earlier invoice mentions an evaporator. It doesn't say the entire refrigeration system was replaced.
  2. Cafe manager: I thought evaporator was another name for the complete cabinet and cooling equipment.
  3. Technician: It's a component where refrigerant absorbs heat, not the whole assembled system.
  4. Cafe manager: That helps. The owner asked whether we bought a completely new machine last year.
  5. Technician: This invoice alone doesn't establish that. We should compare its scope with the asset record.
  6. Cafe manager: I'll retrieve the original purchase record and keep the component invoice separate.
  7. Technician: Please include the cabinet's asset reference so we don't confuse it with the adjacent unit.
  8. Cafe manager: Both cabinets look identical, but their labels in our register are FR-1 and FR-2.
  9. Technician: Use those references in the enquiry; identical appearance doesn't connect their histories.
  10. Cafe manager: I'll ask about FR-2 specifically and avoid describing component work as a full replacement.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Contrast system, component, model, serial, and served area without merging those fields.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Identifying equipment and linked components
Original fictional case: A school service request calls the system AC-4. The roof plan labels an outdoor unit CU-4, while the indoor equipment register identifies air handler AH-4. Their relationship is not confirmed. Facilities manager Mira has the equipment register and discusses the request with service coordinator Owen. They need to preserve the three source references and seek confirmation through the records process. Neither person needs to access the roof or inspect equipment to clarify the existing documents.
Language workshop: AC-4, CU-4, and AH-4 are not yet a pairing
Communication goal: Reconcile conflicting equipment references using existing records and preserve an unverified relationship between components.
Language guidance: AC-4 appears in the request, CU-4 on the roof plan, and AH-4 in the indoor register. Keep all three with their sources. A shared numeral does not confirm a component pairing or served area.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The service request calls the system AC-4. / The roof plan labels an outdoor unit CU-4. / The indoor register identifies air handler AH-4. / Each label should stay attached to its source. / The shared four does not prove that the components are paired. / The relationship between CU-4 and AH-4 is unverified. / You already have the equipment register. / We need a confirmed cross-reference between these entries. / A system label may not be the identifier of one physical unit. / I will not replace AC-4 with CU-4 by guesswork. / We have not confirmed the served area from these labels. / A model number and a serial number identify different things. / Please retain all three references in the query. / This is a document query, not a request for roof access. / The records name the items but do not confirm their relationship. / We need confirmed mapping before merging the references.
Vocabulary: equipment register: Record identifying equipment and relevant asset information. / asset tag: Assigned identifier used to distinguish an equipment item. / system reference: Label used to identify an overall system. / component reference: Label identifying a particular part of a system. / air handler: Equipment that moves and may condition air in a building system. / outdoor unit: Equipment assembly located outside the served interior. / condensing unit: Assembly associated with refrigerant compression and heat rejection in relevant systems. / indoor unit: Equipment assembly located within the building or served space. / equipment pairing: Confirmed relationship between components intended to operate together. / served area: Space associated with the service from an identified system. / roof plan: Drawing showing relevant roof-level features and equipment. / record source: Document or system from which information was obtained. / cross-reference: Link connecting corresponding entries in different records. / label discrepancy: Difference between identifiers in related information. / model number: Manufacturer identifier for a product design or version. / serial number: Identifier distinguishing an individual manufactured unit. / nameplate: Manufacturer label carrying equipment identification or ratings. / asset hierarchy: Organized relationship between systems and their components. / service request: Record asking for attention to an identified issue or system. / records reconciliation: Process of resolving differences between documented information. / unverified link: Relationship that has not been confirmed. / identification query: Request to clarify which equipment is meant. / record custodian: Person responsible for maintaining or providing a record. / confirmed mapping: Verified correspondence between identifiers or components.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Say each identifier with its source: request AC-4, roof plan CU-4, indoor register AH-4. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Contrast system, component, model, serial, and served area without merging those fields. Complete and check the identification exchange. Retain the unverified relationship rather than pairing items by their numeral.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
