03Explaining airflow and distribution reportsTwo ceiling openings, two functions+

Your goal

Translate supply and return labels into plain language while preserving the difference between a comfort report and an airflow measurement.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Two ceiling openings, two functions

Client Tomas calls both ceiling openings air-conditioning outlets. The current drawing identifies S3 as supply air and R2 as return air. He reports discomfort below S3 but supplies no airflow measurements. Technician Leila explains the labels and records the location of the complaint. The drawing establishes the stated functions, not measured performance. No cause, balancing result, or adjustment is supplied, and the conversation must not prescribe changes to grilles or controls.

Tomas: Client / Leila: Technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which opening does the drawing identify as supply?

Answer and explanation

B. S3

The current drawing explicitly labels S3 as supply air.

2. Where does Tomas report discomfort?

Answer and explanation

B. Below S3

The client names the area below S3 as the complaint location.

3. What is missing?

Answer and explanation

C. Airflow measurements

No measured airflow data are supplied with the discomfort report.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

S3 is labeled supply and R2 return on the current drawing. This tells the reader their stated functions. It does not establish actual flow rate, velocity, balance, or the cause of the discomfort.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
supply air
Air delivered into a served space through the distribution system.identify supply air
return air
Air drawn from a space back toward the air-handling system.identify return air
supply grille
Grille associated with delivery of air into the space.locate the supply grille
return grille
Grille associated with air returning from the space.identify the return grille
diffuser
Air-distribution device designed to spread delivered air.identify the diffuser
register
Grille assembly that may include an airflow-control damper.distinguish the register
duct
Passage used to convey air.identify the duct
ductwork
Network of ducts and associated components.review the ductwork
air distribution
Delivery and movement of air within a served area.describe air distribution
airflow rate
Volume of air passing a point per unit time.report the airflow rate
cubic feet per minute
Volume-flow unit commonly abbreviated CFM.state cubic feet per minute
liters per second
Volume-flow unit used for air or liquid movement.record liters per second
air velocity
Speed of air movement at a stated position.distinguish air velocity
air changes per hour
Hourly airflow divided by room volume, with the air source specified.distinguish total and outdoor-air changes
draft
Air movement perceived as unwanted by an occupant.describe a reported draft
occupied zone
Part of a room normally used by people.identify the occupied zone
throw
Distance associated with a defined air jet under stated conditions.review diffuser throw
spread
Lateral extent of a delivered air stream under defined conditions.distinguish spread from throw
damper
Component used to regulate or control airflow in a system.identify the damper
balancing
Technical adjustment and verification of distribution to meet specified requirements.distinguish balancing from explanation
design airflow
Airflow value specified by the design.compare design airflow
measured airflow
Airflow established through the relevant measurement.record measured airflow
distribution report
Account describing air delivery or related comfort observations.clarify the distribution report
performance finding
Supported conclusion about how a system functions.distinguish a performance finding

The report concerns discomfort below S3. Preserve that location without changing it to a measured excess or deficit of airflow. Explanation of the labels is not an instruction to adjust a grille, damper, or control.

C Notice the language

Translate the function, then return to the complaint

An everyday label can conceal an important functional difference. Explain the direction of airflow simply, then repeat the actual complaint. Technical vocabulary should improve the report, not overwhelm the customer or imply a diagnosis that the measurements do not support.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the two functions
S3 is supply air; R2 is return air.
Explain supply
Supply air is delivered into the room.
Explain return
Return air goes back toward the air-handling system.
Attribute the labels
Those are the functions shown on the current drawing.
Keep the complaint location
You report discomfort below S3.
Avoid converting experience into measurement
We do not have an airflow measurement for that location.
Separate rate and speed
Airflow rate and air velocity are different quantities.
Name common units
Volume flow may be stated in cubic feet per minute or liters per second.
Avoid a balance claim
The labels do not establish that the system is balanced.
Keep cause open
The cause of the discomfort has not been established.
Clarify the everyday term
Calling both openings outlets hides their different functions.
Retain the customer's words
I will record the discomfort without inventing a measured excess.
Avoid prescribing a change
This explanation does not specify an adjustment.
Ask for the existing reference
Please keep S3 attached to the reported location.
Distinguish design from result
A design value is not automatically a measured result.
Close with a precise report
Discomfort below supply S3; airflow data not supplied; cause unresolved.
6 language and precision notes
Shown on the drawing
Attributes the stated function without verifying installed performance.
Into versus back toward
Gives the customer a simple directional contrast.
Below S3
Preserves the complaint location rather than generalizing to the whole system.
Not automatically
Blocks an unsupported equivalence between design and measurement.
Reported discomfort
Records the experience without assigning a technical cause.
Does not specify
Keeps an explanation separate from an operating instruction.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Supply delivers air into the room; return carries air back toward the system

The directional distinction explains the stated functions without changing them into performance findings.

2. What can the drawing labels establish here?

Answer and explanation

C. The stated functions of S3 and R2

The supplied drawing labels identify functions, not measured operating conditions.

3. Which report invents a measurement?

Answer and explanation

D. S3 delivers too many liters per second

No airflow measurements support a quantified excess at S3.

4. What is the proper scope of Leila's response?

Answer and explanation

D. Explain labels and preserve the complaint for assessment

The case supports explanation and accurate reporting, not adjustment or performance conclusions.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Two ceiling openings, two functions

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Tomas: Client / Leila: Technician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Air velocity
  • distribution report
  • occupied zone
  • measured airflow
  • performance finding
  • return air
  • supply air
  • cubic feet per minute
  • damper
  • balancing
  1. Tomas
    Leila, the ceiling outlet above this seat is uncomfortable to sit under. It's S3 on the drawing. The other outlet is R2.
  2. Leila
    S3 is labeled 1 on the drawing. R2 has a different function, so calling both outlets can hide a useful distinction.
  3. Tomas
    They look like parts of the same system. Does R2 also send air into the room, just from a different position?
  4. Leila
    R2 is labeled 2. Supply delivers air into the space; return carries air back toward the air-handling system. Return isn't automatically an outdoor exhaust.
  5. Tomas
    Then the complaint belongs below the supply opening, S3. Please keep that location rather than writing that the whole room's ventilation has failed.
  6. Leila
    I'll retain it in the 3: discomfort below S3. We can identify the drawing label without claiming that it verifies how the installed system performs.
  7. Tomas
    I was about to say S3 delivers too much air. That's how it feels here, but I don't have a measured figure to attach.
  8. Leila
    Keep it as your experience, then. A 4 value needs measurement evidence. We shouldn't turn a comfort report into an unsupported volume-flow result.
  9. Tomas
    An earlier report used CFM. I assumed that was how fast the air moves. Does it actually describe something different?
  10. Leila
    It means 5, a volume per unit time. It describes how much air volume moves, not the speed at one point by itself.
  11. Tomas
    So a feeling of air movement at my seat doesn't give us a CFM figure. And liters per second would be another volume-flow unit.
  12. Leila
    Correct. 6 is speed at a location, distinct from volume flow. Neither measurement is supplied in this report, although your location description is useful.
  13. Tomas
    Since we know which opening is the return, can we at least say the system is balanced? The drawing appears to show a complete arrangement.
  14. Leila
    Identifying the openings doesn't establish 7. That involves technical distribution work and verification, not simply checking that supply and return labels exist.
  15. Tomas
    Someone suggested closing a grille to stop the discomfort. I don't want the service request to present that suggestion as the approved remedy.
  16. Leila
    It won't. No 8 adjustment or grille change is prescribed by these records. The appropriate assessment needs to establish the condition and any required response.
  17. Tomas
    Please include that it's the seat below S3 that concerns me. Saying air-conditioning problem leaves the person reading it with very little to locate.
  18. Leila
    Agreed. The 9 location matters to the report. We'll preserve where you experience discomfort without naming an equipment fault that hasn't been established.
  19. Tomas
    Read me the short version, please. I'll use that wording when the facilities manager asks why the request was raised.
  20. Leila
    Discomfort reported below supply S3; R2 labeled return; no airflow measurements supplied. No 10 or cause is established. That keeps the complaint precise without inventing results.

Answers and explanations
  1. supply air Turn 2. Supply air is the stated delivery function of S3 on the current drawing.
  2. return air Turn 4. Return air describes the flow back toward the system rather than delivery into the room.
  3. distribution report Turn 6. Distribution report records the air-related complaint and its location without adding measurements.
  4. measured airflow Turn 8. Measured airflow would require relevant measurement data, which Tomas has not supplied.
  5. cubic feet per minute Turn 10. Cubic feet per minute is a volume-flow unit, not an air-speed unit.
  6. Air velocity Turn 12. Air velocity is the speed of air movement and must not be confused with volume flow.
  7. balancing Turn 14. Balancing involves technical distribution work and verification, not merely identifying supply and return labels.
  8. damper Turn 16. Damper is an airflow-control component; no adjustment to it is supported by this conversation.
  9. occupied zone Turn 18. Occupied zone is the area used by people, helping locate the comfort issue rather than diagnose equipment.
  10. performance finding Turn 20. Performance finding would require supporting evidence beyond the labels and discomfort account.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Tomas: Leila, the ceiling outlet above this seat is uncomfortable to sit under. It's S3 on the drawing. The other outlet is R2.
  2. Leila: S3 is labeled supply air on the drawing. R2 has a different function, so calling both outlets can hide a useful distinction.
  3. Tomas: They look like parts of the same system. Does R2 also send air into the room, just from a different position?
  4. Leila: R2 is labeled return air. Supply delivers air into the space; return carries air back toward the air-handling system. Return isn't automatically an outdoor exhaust.
  5. Tomas: Then the complaint belongs below the supply opening, S3. Please keep that location rather than writing that the whole room's ventilation has failed.
  6. Leila: I'll retain it in the distribution report: discomfort below S3. We can identify the drawing label without claiming that it verifies how the installed system performs.
  7. Tomas: I was about to say S3 delivers too much air. That's how it feels here, but I don't have a measured figure to attach.
  8. Leila: Keep it as your experience, then. A measured airflow value needs measurement evidence. We shouldn't turn a comfort report into an unsupported volume-flow result.
  9. Tomas: An earlier report used CFM. I assumed that was how fast the air moves. Does it actually describe something different?
  10. Leila: It means cubic feet per minute, a volume per unit time. It describes how much air volume moves, not the speed at one point by itself.
  11. Tomas: So a feeling of air movement at my seat doesn't give us a CFM figure. And liters per second would be another volume-flow unit.
  12. Leila: Correct. Air velocity is speed at a location, distinct from volume flow. Neither measurement is supplied in this report, although your location description is useful.
  13. Tomas: Since we know which opening is the return, can we at least say the system is balanced? The drawing appears to show a complete arrangement.
  14. Leila: Identifying the openings doesn't establish balancing. That involves technical distribution work and verification, not simply checking that supply and return labels exist.
  15. Tomas: Someone suggested closing a grille to stop the discomfort. I don't want the service request to present that suggestion as the approved remedy.
  16. Leila: It won't. No damper adjustment or grille change is prescribed by these records. The appropriate assessment needs to establish the condition and any required response.
  17. Tomas: Please include that it's the seat below S3 that concerns me. Saying air-conditioning problem leaves the person reading it with very little to locate.
  18. Leila: Agreed. The occupied zone location matters to the report. We'll preserve where you experience discomfort without naming an equipment fault that hasn't been established.
  19. Tomas: Read me the short version, please. I'll use that wording when the facilities manager asks why the request was raised.
  20. Leila: Discomfort reported below supply S3; R2 labeled return; no airflow measurements supplied. No performance finding or cause is established. That keeps the complaint precise without inventing results.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Supply S3 and return R2

A technician explains two ceiling references to a client.

  1. Technician: The drawing identifies S3 as supply air and R2 as return air.
  2. Client: I called both outlets because they look similar from the meeting table.
  3. Technician: Their functions differ: S3 delivers air, while R2 draws air back toward the system.
  4. Client: The seat below S3 feels uncomfortable. Does that prove there's too much airflow?
  5. Technician: It locates the complaint, but it doesn't establish a measured airflow or its cause.
  6. Client: Then please give the reviewer that seat location rather than just saying the room is uncomfortable.
  7. Technician: I will include S3 and the occupied position, with your report clearly attributed.
  8. Client: Can the earlier balancing report help them compare the intended distribution with the records?
  9. Technician: Yes, if its locations and scope match; we'll request it for the review.
  10. Client: I'll provide the seating plan so the report refers to the place people actually use.
Different units on the two reports

A technician checks a comparison with a facilities analyst.

  1. Technician: These airflow reports use different units, and their measurement locations aren't the same.
  2. Facilities analyst: I compared the numbers directly and concluded the new value was much lower.
  3. Technician: That comparison isn't established without reconciling units, locations, and the stated test conditions.
  4. Facilities analyst: I'll withdraw the conclusion and ask the report author for a comparable summary.
  5. Technician: Please keep the original values and units intact rather than changing them informally.
  6. Facilities analyst: One report also lists static pressure. Can I use that instead of missing airflow?
  7. Technician: They describe different quantities; a pressure figure isn't a substitute for an airflow reading.
  8. Facilities analyst: Understood. I'll identify the missing airflow information and keep pressure in its own field.
  9. Technician: The technical reviewer can interpret the relationship using the complete records and their limitations.
  10. Facilities analyst: I'll send the corrected comparison request without treating unlike numbers as evidence of declining performance.
The return opening is missing from the furniture plan

A technician and interior coordinator review a new office arrangement.

  1. Technician: The mechanical drawing identifies a return-air opening where the furniture plan shows a tall cabinet.
  2. Interior coordinator: The furniture team treated that wall as empty because the opening wasn't on their elevation.
  3. Technician: We need the two layouts reviewed together before confirming the cabinet position.
  4. Interior coordinator: Can we describe this as a coordination conflict rather than an instruction to move the opening?
  5. Technician: Yes. Identify the return reference and proposed cabinet dimensions for the design team.
  6. Interior coordinator: I'll ask them to assess the relationship, with access and airflow requirements considered specifically.
  7. Technician: Please avoid saying a decorative grille elsewhere would automatically provide an equivalent arrangement.
  8. Interior coordinator: I won't. Similar appearance doesn't establish the same system function or performance.
  9. Technician: The client can still express an appearance preference while the technical layout remains under review.
  10. Interior coordinator: I'll request a coordinated option that addresses both requirements before the cabinet order is released.

F Say it

Two ceiling openings, two functions
  1. Read turns 1-10. Use clear contrastive stress for supply into the room and return back toward the system.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Distinguish CFM volume flow from air velocity and a comfort report from a measured result.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Answer and explanation

D. supply

Answer and explanation

A. return

Return distinguishes R2 from the supply opening named in the complaint.

Answer and explanation

S3 is the specific location identified by the client.

Answer and explanation

A. measurements

The complaint includes no measured airflow data to support a performance conclusion.

Lesson 03: Explaining airflow and distribution reports
Original fictional case: Client Tomas calls both ceiling openings air-conditioning outlets. The current drawing identifies S3 as supply air and R2 as return air. He reports discomfort below S3 but supplies no airflow measurements. Technician Leila explains the labels and records the location of the complaint. The drawing establishes the stated functions, not measured performance. No cause, balancing result, or adjustment is supplied, and the conversation must not prescribe changes to grilles or controls.
Language workshop: Two ceiling openings, two functions
Communication goal: Translate supply and return labels into plain language while preserving the difference between a comfort report and an airflow measurement.
Language guidance: S3 is labeled supply and R2 return on the current drawing. This tells the reader their stated functions. It does not establish actual flow rate, velocity, balance, or the cause of the discomfort.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): S3 is supply air; R2 is return air. / Supply air is delivered into the room. / Return air goes back toward the air-handling system. / Those are the functions shown on the current drawing. / You report discomfort below S3. / We do not have an airflow measurement for that location. / Airflow rate and air velocity are different quantities. / Volume flow may be stated in cubic feet per minute or liters per second. / The labels do not establish that the system is balanced. / The cause of the discomfort has not been established. / Calling both openings outlets hides their different functions. / I will record the discomfort without inventing a measured excess. / This explanation does not specify an adjustment. / Please keep S3 attached to the reported location. / A design value is not automatically a measured result. / Discomfort below supply S3; airflow data not supplied; cause unresolved.
Vocabulary: supply air: Air delivered into a served space through the distribution system. / return air: Air drawn from a space back toward the air-handling system. / supply grille: Grille associated with delivery of air into the space. / return grille: Grille associated with air returning from the space. / diffuser: Air-distribution device designed to spread delivered air. / register: Grille assembly that may include an airflow-control damper. / duct: Passage used to convey air. / ductwork: Network of ducts and associated components. / air distribution: Delivery and movement of air within a served area. / airflow rate: Volume of air passing a point per unit time. / cubic feet per minute: Volume-flow unit commonly abbreviated CFM. / liters per second: Volume-flow unit used for air or liquid movement. / air velocity: Speed of air movement at a stated position. / air changes per hour: Hourly airflow divided by room volume, with the air source specified. / draft: Air movement perceived as unwanted by an occupant. / occupied zone: Part of a room normally used by people. / throw: Distance associated with a defined air jet under stated conditions. / spread: Lateral extent of a delivered air stream under defined conditions. / damper: Component used to regulate or control airflow in a system. / balancing: Technical adjustment and verification of distribution to meet specified requirements. / design airflow: Airflow value specified by the design. / measured airflow: Airflow established through the relevant measurement. / distribution report: Account describing air delivery or related comfort observations. / performance finding: Supported conclusion about how a system functions.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Checking equipment ratings and refrigerant referencesFive kilowatts of what?+

Your goal

Compare like quantities, request missing equipment data, and distinguish refrigerant identification from approval to select or service a system.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Five kilowatts of what?

Client Jules compares two fictional proposals with estimator Farah. Both show 5 kilowatts, but proposal A labels the value cooling capacity while proposal B labels it electrical input. Jules calls them equal-size systems. Full data sheets and equipment pairings are not supplied, and no selection is approved. Farah must explain why the matching numbers are not enough, request complete specifications, and keep refrigerant references tied to the actual equipment without recommending handling or substitution.

Jules: Client / Farah: Estimator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does proposal A's 5 kilowatts describe?

Answer and explanation

C. Cooling capacity

Proposal A explicitly labels the value as cooling capacity.

2. What does proposal B's 5 kilowatts describe?

Answer and explanation

D. Electrical input

Proposal B labels the value electrical input, which is a different quantity.

3. What conclusion is supported?

Answer and explanation

C. The numbers alone cannot establish equivalent systems

The matching unit and value do not make the two differently labeled quantities equivalent.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Both proposals state 5 kilowatts, but A describes cooling output and B electrical input. The unit can express either quantity. It does not follow that the systems have equal cooling capacities or equal efficiencies.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
cooling capacity
Rate at which a system can remove heat under stated conditions.compare cooling capacity
electrical input
Electrical power required by the equipment under stated conditions.identify electrical input
kilowatt
Unit of power equal to one thousand watts.state the rating in kilowatts
kilowatt-hour
Unit of energy representing one kilowatt over one hour.distinguish a kilowatt-hour
rating condition
Defined condition under which a performance value is stated.check the rating conditions
data sheet
Manufacturer document listing relevant product characteristics.obtain the complete data sheet
equipment combination
Particular set of components proposed to work together.verify the equipment combination
matched system
Component arrangement confirmed as suitable together by the relevant specifications.identify the matched system
nominal capacity
Stated classification or approximate rating rather than every operating output.distinguish nominal capacity
operating condition
Relevant environmental or system state during operation.state the operating conditions
efficiency
Relationship between useful output and required input under defined conditions.compare efficiency on a consistent basis
coefficient of performance
Ratio of useful heating or cooling output to input power on a defined basis.interpret the coefficient of performance
refrigerant
Working fluid used to transfer heat in a refrigeration system.identify the specified refrigerant
refrigerant designation
Identifier naming a particular refrigerant or blend.preserve the refrigerant designation
refrigerant charge
Quantity of refrigerant specified or present in a system.distinguish refrigerant charge
blend
Refrigerant made from more than one component substance.identify a refrigerant blend
compatibility
Suitability of materials or components for use together.verify compatibility
manufacturer specification
Product requirement or attribute stated by its manufacturer.consult the manufacturer specification
superheat
Vapor temperature above its saturation reference at the same pressure; use dew for the stated blend.report superheat in kelvin
selection basis
Information and criteria supporting an equipment choice.establish the selection basis
like-for-like comparison
Comparison of matching quantities on a consistent basis.make a like-for-like comparison
sensible heat ratio
Sensible cooling capacity divided by total cooling capacity on the same basis.calculate the sensible heat ratio
subcooling
Liquid temperature below its saturation reference at the same pressure; use bubble for the stated blend.distinguish subcooling from superheat
substitution review
Evaluation of a proposed replacement for a specified item.request a substitution review

The complete data sheets and component pairings are missing. A refrigerant designation must stay tied to the actual equipment specification; a familiar refrigerant name does not approve a substitution, determine compatibility, or authorize servicing.

C Notice the language

Ask what a familiar number measures

Customers often compare the largest printed number in a proposal. Name the quantity before comparing the value. Explain what information is missing and why it matters, without turning a partial specification into a product recommendation or an efficiency claim.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask the key question
Five kilowatts of which quantity?
Read proposal A
A lists 5 kilowatts of cooling capacity.
Read proposal B
B lists 5 kilowatts of electrical input.
Name the distinction
Cooling output and electrical input are different.
Reject the false equivalence
Matching numbers do not establish equal-size systems here.
Ask for complete data
We need the full data sheets for both proposals.
Include the combination
The proposed component pairings also need to be identified.
Keep conditions attached
Compare ratings under the relevant stated conditions.
Avoid inventing efficiency
We cannot calculate a supported efficiency comparison from these two entries.
Distinguish power and energy
Kilowatts and kilowatt-hours are different units.
Keep refrigerant information specific
Use the refrigerant designation from the actual equipment specification.
Avoid treating a name as approval
A refrigerant reference does not approve a substitution.
Keep charge separate
Refrigerant type and refrigerant quantity are different information.
Name the review task
We need a like-for-like comparison.
State the selection limit
No equipment selection has been approved.
Close with the request
Please supply complete ratings and equipment combinations for review.
6 language and precision notes
Of which quantity?
Forces a value to be connected to what it describes.
Both ... but
Acknowledges the shared number while exposing the important difference.
Under stated conditions
Prevents ratings from being treated as universal operating results.
Cannot calculate from these entries
Limits a conclusion to the information actually available.
Type versus quantity
Separates refrigerant identity from charge amount.
No selection approved
Keeps information gathering distinct from a purchase or installation decision.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which comparison is valid from the supplied entries?

Answer and explanation

C. A's value is cooling capacity; B's is electrical input

Only the differently labeled quantities can be identified; performance equivalence is not established.

2. What additional information is needed?

Answer and explanation

C. Full data sheets, rating conditions, and component pairings

Matching quantities, conditions, and actual equipment combinations are needed; headline numbers or refrigerant names do not fill those gaps.

3. Which conclusion exceeds the evidence?

Answer and explanation

A. The systems have equal efficiency

The two entries do not provide the corresponding output and input data needed for that claim.

4. What does a refrigerant designation provide?

Answer and explanation

C. Identification to be checked against the actual equipment specification

The designation identifies the fluid but does not establish compatibility or authorization.

E Conversations

Five kilowatts of what?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jules: Client / Farah: Estimator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • refrigerant designation
  • data sheet
  • like-for-like comparison
  • equipment combination
  • cooling capacity
  • substitution review
  • selection basis
  • efficiency
  • Refrigerant charge
  • Electrical input
  1. Jules
    Farah, both proposals show five kilowatts. I put equal-size systems in my comparison sheet and moved straight to the two prices.
  2. Farah
    Let's check five kilowatts of what. Proposal A labels it 1. B uses a different quantity label even though its number and unit look the same.
  3. Jules
    B says electrical input. I treated kilowatts as though they always told us how much heat the system could remove from the room.
  4. Farah
    2 is the power required by the equipment. Cooling capacity is heat-removal output under stated conditions, so these entries don't establish equal output.
  5. Jules
    Could I divide A's five by B's five and report a performance ratio of one? They're both power figures, after all.
  6. Farah
    Not for an 3 comparison. You would be combining one system's output with another system's input, rather than corresponding quantities for each proposed arrangement.
  7. Jules
    Then asking each supplier to confirm five kilowatts again wouldn't fix it. We need both quantity labels and the missing values for each proposal.
  8. Farah
    Yes, request each full 4, with the proposed components and rating conditions. A short headline doesn't establish performance across every operating situation.
  9. Jules
    I haven't received the indoor and outdoor pairings. Is a brochure for the outdoor unit enough if the installer chooses the indoor one later?
  10. Farah
    No. The 5 matters to the stated performance. We need to know which components the proposal actually pairs, not assume any indoor unit gives the same result.
  11. Jules
    One summary includes a refrigerant name. Could I use that as evidence that the two proposals are interchangeable replacements?
  12. Farah
    A 6 identifies a fluid or blend, not an equivalent system. It must be checked against the actual equipment specification and doesn't settle all compatibility requirements.
  13. Jules
    I don't want the supplier to read our question as permission to use another fluid. We're asking for information, not changing the specified equipment.
  14. Farah
    Then keep any 7 separate and explicitly pending. A familiar refrigerant name doesn't bypass that process or authorize refrigerant handling.
  15. Jules
    The sheet also has a refrigerant amount. Is that a different statement of capacity, or is it simply another product detail?
  16. Farah
    8 is the quantity of refrigerant specified or present. Keep the type and amount separate from cooling capacity and electrical input.
  17. Jules
    I see four different things now: fluid identity, fluid quantity, output power, and input power. The matching fives only concern two of those.
  18. Farah
    Right. We need a 9 using corresponding ratings and conditions. None of the missing values should be filled with the figure from the other proposal.
  19. Jules
    I'll remove equal-size and ask for complete data sheets, actual pairings, and both output and input values with their conditions.
  20. Farah
    That improves the 10. It doesn't approve either option yet, but it gives the technical review the information needed to make a meaningful comparison.

Answers and explanations
  1. cooling capacity Turn 2. Cooling capacity identifies A's heat-removal output rating, not its electrical power input.
  2. Electrical input Turn 4. Electrical input is the power required by the equipment, not the useful cooling output.
  3. efficiency Turn 6. Efficiency comparison needs corresponding output and input information on a consistent basis.
  4. data sheet Turn 8. Data sheet supplies product characteristics beyond the two incomplete proposal entries.
  5. equipment combination Turn 10. Equipment combination identifies the components whose joint specifications are relevant to the proposal.
  6. refrigerant designation Turn 12. Refrigerant designation identifies the specified fluid but does not establish system equivalence or replacement approval.
  7. substitution review Turn 14. Substitution review evaluates a proposed replacement and is not bypassed by a familiar refrigerant name.
  8. Refrigerant charge Turn 16. Refrigerant charge concerns the quantity of working fluid, distinct from capacity and refrigerant identity.
  9. like-for-like comparison Turn 18. Like-for-like comparison uses matching quantities and conditions instead of the superficially matching five-kilowatt figures.
  10. selection basis Turn 20. Selection basis is the supporting information still needed before an equipment choice can be approved.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Jules: Farah, both proposals show five kilowatts. I put equal-size systems in my comparison sheet and moved straight to the two prices.
  2. Farah: Let's check five kilowatts of what. Proposal A labels it cooling capacity. B uses a different quantity label even though its number and unit look the same.
  3. Jules: B says electrical input. I treated kilowatts as though they always told us how much heat the system could remove from the room.
  4. Farah: Electrical input is the power required by the equipment. Cooling capacity is heat-removal output under stated conditions, so these entries don't establish equal output.
  5. Jules: Could I divide A's five by B's five and report a performance ratio of one? They're both power figures, after all.
  6. Farah: Not for an efficiency comparison. You would be combining one system's output with another system's input, rather than corresponding quantities for each proposed arrangement.
  7. Jules: Then asking each supplier to confirm five kilowatts again wouldn't fix it. We need both quantity labels and the missing values for each proposal.
  8. Farah: Yes, request each full data sheet, with the proposed components and rating conditions. A short headline doesn't establish performance across every operating situation.
  9. Jules: I haven't received the indoor and outdoor pairings. Is a brochure for the outdoor unit enough if the installer chooses the indoor one later?
  10. Farah: No. The equipment combination matters to the stated performance. We need to know which components the proposal actually pairs, not assume any indoor unit gives the same result.
  11. Jules: One summary includes a refrigerant name. Could I use that as evidence that the two proposals are interchangeable replacements?
  12. Farah: A refrigerant designation identifies a fluid or blend, not an equivalent system. It must be checked against the actual equipment specification and doesn't settle all compatibility requirements.
  13. Jules: I don't want the supplier to read our question as permission to use another fluid. We're asking for information, not changing the specified equipment.
  14. Farah: Then keep any substitution review separate and explicitly pending. A familiar refrigerant name doesn't bypass that process or authorize refrigerant handling.
  15. Jules: The sheet also has a refrigerant amount. Is that a different statement of capacity, or is it simply another product detail?
  16. Farah: Refrigerant charge is the quantity of refrigerant specified or present. Keep the type and amount separate from cooling capacity and electrical input.

Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Five kilowatts of what?

A technician compares two incomplete proposals with a client.

  1. Technician: Proposal A lists five kilowatts of cooling capacity; B lists five kilowatts electrical input.
  2. Client: I thought the matching numbers meant both systems had the same cooling output.
  3. Technician: They describe different quantities, even though both use kilowatts as the unit.
  4. Client: Then we need B's cooling-capacity rating before making that particular comparison.
  5. Technician: Yes, with the rating conditions and the complete proposed indoor and outdoor components.
  6. Client: The seller says both use the same refrigerant designation. Does that settle compatibility?
  7. Technician: No, the fluid identifier alone doesn't establish a matched system or equivalent performance.
  8. Client: I'll request the complete data sheets rather than repeat the equal-size claim.
  9. Technician: Please also separate the equipment comparison from any prediction about this building's cooling demand.
  10. Client: Understood. We can review the actual ratings first, with selection and suitability still open.
A missing suffix on the nameplate record

A technician clarifies a replacement enquiry with a parts coordinator.

  1. Technician: The equipment record omits the model suffix shown in the existing nameplate photograph.
  2. Parts coordinator: I shortened the reference because the supplier's search found several similar models.
  3. Technician: That suffix may identify a different variant, so the complete reference belongs in the enquiry.
  4. Parts coordinator: I'll use the photograph already in the records, without asking anyone to access equipment.
  5. Technician: Please retain the serial number too, clearly separated from the model and asset tag.
  6. Parts coordinator: The supplier asked for the refrigerant designation. Is that another serial-number field?
  7. Technician: No, it identifies the specified working fluid. Copy the documented label accurately without inferring compatibility.
  8. Parts coordinator: I'll send the complete recorded information and flag any unreadable field instead of guessing.
  9. Technician: That lets the supplier assess the correct equipment rather than a nearby catalog match.
  10. Parts coordinator: I'll keep the proposed replacement pending until its exact applicability is confirmed.
The outdoor unit alone has a quoted rating

A technician and purchaser discuss an incomplete equipment quotation.

  1. Technician: This quotation identifies the outdoor unit, but the indoor component reference is missing.
  2. Purchaser: The salesperson gave us an efficiency claim and said the indoor unit was standard.
  3. Technician: We need the actual combination and its rating basis before treating that claim as applicable.
  4. Purchaser: I'll ask for the complete matched-system reference rather than a generic indoor-unit description.
  5. Technician: Please include the conditions associated with any stated capacity or efficiency figure.
  6. Purchaser: The client wants to compare running costs. Can we use the catalog headline directly?
  7. Technician: A headline rating alone doesn't establish this site's energy use or costs.
  8. Purchaser: Then I'll present it as product information, not a guaranteed saving for the building.
  9. Technician: That keeps the comparison honest while the specialist reviews the complete proposed equipment combination.
  10. Purchaser: I'll request the missing references and preserve the original quotation as incomplete pending clarification.

Two scenarios to practice

Five kilowatts of what?
  1. Read turns 1-10. Attach cooling capacity to A and electrical input to B every time five kilowatts is mentioned.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep refrigerant designation and charge separate, and make the missing pairing explicit.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep input and output apart

Complete the proposal comparison. Preserve the different quantity labels and the missing approval.

Answer and explanation

A. capacity

Capacity identifies the cooling-output quantity stated in proposal A.

2. Estimator: "B gives 5 kilowatts of electrical ___."

Answer and explanation

D. input

Input identifies power required by the equipment, not cooling output.

Answer and explanation

C. sheets

Full data sheets and component pairings are missing from the supplied proposals.

4. Estimator: "No selection is yet ___."

Answer and explanation

C. approved

The comparison does not establish an accepted equipment choice or authorization.

Professional audience: Heating, ventilation, air-conditioning, and refrigeration technicians, supervised apprentices, service coordinators, and mechanical crew leads
Scope: The projects, products, values, and records are fictional. Lesson 04: Checking equipment ratings and refrigerant references
Original fictional case: Client Jules compares two fictional proposals with estimator Farah. Both show 5 kilowatts, but proposal A labels the value cooling capacity while proposal B labels it electrical input. Jules calls them equal-size systems. Full data sheets and equipment pairings are not supplied, and no selection is approved. Farah must explain why the matching numbers are not enough, request complete specifications, and keep refrigerant references tied to the actual equipment without recommending handling or substitution.
Language workshop: Five kilowatts of what?
Communication goal: Compare like quantities, request missing equipment data, and distinguish refrigerant identification from approval to select or service a system.
Language guidance: Both proposals state 5 kilowatts, but A describes cooling output and B electrical input. The unit can express either quantity. It does not follow that the systems have equal cooling capacities or equal efficiencies.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Five kilowatts of which quantity? / A lists 5 kilowatts of cooling capacity. / B lists 5 kilowatts of electrical input. / Cooling output and electrical input are different. / Matching numbers do not establish equal-size systems here. / We need the full data sheets for both proposals. / The proposed component pairings also need to be identified. / Compare ratings under the relevant stated conditions. / We cannot calculate a supported efficiency comparison from these two entries. / Kilowatts and kilowatt-hours are different units. / Use the refrigerant designation from the actual equipment specification. / A refrigerant reference does not approve a substitution. / Refrigerant type and refrigerant quantity are different information. / We need a like-for-like comparison. / No equipment selection has been approved. / Please supply complete ratings and equipment combinations for review.
Vocabulary: cooling capacity: Rate at which a system can remove heat under stated conditions. / electrical input: Electrical power required by the equipment under stated conditions. / kilowatt: Unit of power equal to one thousand watts. / kilowatt-hour: Unit of energy representing one kilowatt over one hour. / rating condition: Defined condition under which a performance value is stated. / data sheet: Manufacturer document listing relevant product characteristics. / equipment combination: Particular set of components proposed to work together. / matched system: Component arrangement confirmed as suitable together by the relevant specifications. / nominal capacity: Stated classification or approximate rating rather than every operating output. / operating condition: Relevant environmental or system state during operation. / efficiency: Relationship between useful output and required input under defined conditions. / coefficient of performance: Ratio of useful heating or cooling output to input power on a defined basis. / refrigerant: Working fluid used to transfer heat in a refrigeration system. / refrigerant designation: Identifier naming a particular refrigerant or blend. / refrigerant charge: Quantity of refrigerant specified or present in a system. / blend: Refrigerant made from more than one component substance. / compatibility: Suitability of materials or components for use together. / manufacturer specification: Product requirement or attribute stated by its manufacturer. / superheat: Vapor temperature above its saturation reference at the same pressure; use dew for the stated blend. / selection basis: Information and criteria supporting an equipment choice. / like-for-like comparison: Comparison of matching quantities on a consistent basis. / sensible heat ratio: Sensible cooling capacity divided by total cooling capacity on the same basis. / subcooling: Liquid temperature below its saturation reference at the same pressure; use bubble for the stated blend. / substitution review: Evaluation of a proposed replacement for a specified item.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Attach cooling capacity to A and electrical input to B every time five kilowatts is mentioned. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep refrigerant designation and charge separate, and make the missing pairing explicit. Complete and check the comparison exchange. Request complete data without approving equipment or calculating across different proposals.
Partner: Jules: Client / Farah: Estimator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the proposal comparison. Preserve the different quantity labels and the missing approval.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jules: I see four different things now: fluid identity, fluid quantity, output power, and input power. The matching fives only concern two of those.
Farah: Right. We need a like-for-like comparison using corresponding ratings and conditions. None of the missing values should be filled with the figure from the other proposal.
Jules: I'll remove equal-size and ask for complete data sheets, actual pairings, and both output and input values with their conditions.
Farah: That improves the selection basis. It doesn't approve either option yet, but it gives the technical review the information needed to make a meaningful comparison.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: HVAC & Refrigeration English
Professional audience: Heating, ventilation, air-conditioning, and refrigeration technicians, supervised apprentices, service coordinators, and mechanical crew leads
Scope: The projects, products, values, and records are fictional. This book teaches English, not equipment operation, diagnosis, installation, refrigerant handling, food-safety decisions, or compliance. Follow actual training, authorization, manufacturer instructions, site procedures, and applicable requirements. No dialogue authorizes roof access, testing, adjustment, refrigerant work, food release, or a system alteration. Equipment readings and document receipt do not establish safety or overall performance.
Lesson 04: Checking equipment ratings and refrigerant references
Original fictional case: Client Jules compares two fictional proposals with estimator Farah. Both show 5 kilowatts, but proposal A labels the value cooling capacity while proposal B labels it electrical input. Jules calls them equal-size systems. Full data sheets and equipment pairings are not supplied, and no selection is approved. Farah must explain why the matching numbers are not enough, request complete specifications, and keep refrigerant references tied to the actual equipment without recommending handling or substitution.
Language workshop: Five kilowatts of what?
Communication goal: Compare like quantities, request missing equipment data, and distinguish refrigerant identification from approval to select or service a system.
Language guidance: Both proposals state 5 kilowatts, but A describes cooling output and B electrical input. The unit can express either quantity. It does not follow that the systems have equal cooling capacities or equal efficiencies.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Five kilowatts of which quantity? / A lists 5 kilowatts of cooling capacity. / B lists 5 kilowatts of electrical input. / Cooling output and electrical input are different. / Matching numbers do not establish equal-size systems here. / We need the full data sheets for both proposals. / The proposed component pairings also need to be identified. / Compare ratings under the relevant stated conditions. / We cannot calculate a supported efficiency comparison from these two entries. / Kilowatts and kilowatt-hours are different units. / Use the refrigerant designation from the actual equipment specification. / A refrigerant reference does not approve a substitution. / Refrigerant type and refrigerant quantity are different information. / We need a like-for-like comparison. / No equipment selection has been approved. / Please supply complete ratings and equipment combinations for review.
Vocabulary: cooling capacity: Rate at which a system can remove heat under stated conditions. / electrical input: Electrical power required by the equipment under stated conditions. / kilowatt: Unit of power equal to one thousand watts. / kilowatt-hour: Unit of energy representing one kilowatt over one hour. / rating condition: Defined condition under which a performance value is stated. / data sheet: Manufacturer document listing relevant product characteristics. / equipment combination: Particular set of components proposed to work together. / matched system: Component arrangement confirmed as suitable together by the relevant specifications. / nominal capacity: Stated classification or approximate rating rather than every operating output. / operating condition: Relevant environmental or system state during operation. / efficiency: Relationship between useful output and required input under defined conditions. / coefficient of performance: Ratio of useful heating or cooling output to input power on a defined basis. / refrigerant: Working fluid used to transfer heat in a refrigeration system. / refrigerant designation: Identifier naming a particular refrigerant or blend. / refrigerant charge: Quantity of refrigerant specified or present in a system. / blend: Refrigerant made from more than one component substance. / compatibility: Suitability of materials or components for use together. / manufacturer specification: Product requirement or attribute stated by its manufacturer. / superheat: Vapor temperature above its saturation reference at the same pressure; use dew for the stated blend. / selection basis: Information and criteria supporting an equipment choice. / like-for-like comparison: Comparison of matching quantities on a consistent basis. / sensible heat ratio: Sensible cooling capacity divided by total cooling capacity on the same basis. / subcooling: Liquid temperature below its saturation reference at the same pressure; use bubble for the stated blend. / substitution review: Evaluation of a proposed replacement for a specified item.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Attach cooling capacity to A and electrical input to B every time five kilowatts is mentioned. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep refrigerant designation and charge separate, and make the missing pairing explicit. Complete and check the comparison exchange. Request complete data without approving equipment or calculating across different proposals.
Partner: Jules: Client / Farah: Estimator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the proposal comparison. Preserve the different quantity labels and the missing approval.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jules: I see four different things now: fluid identity, fluid quantity, output power, and input power. The matching fives only concern two of those.
Farah: Right. We need a like-for-like comparison using corresponding ratings and conditions. None of the missing values should be filled with the figure from the other proposal.
Jules: I'll remove equal-size and ask for complete data sheets, actual pairings, and both output and input values with their conditions.
Farah: That improves the selection basis. It doesn't approve either option yet, but it gives the technical review the information needed to make a meaningful comparison.
END REFERENCE
05Reporting moisture, symptoms, and service findingsYesterday wet, today dry, source still unknown+

Your goal

Reconcile a historical report with a later observation and avoid treating an earlier maintenance concern as a proven cause.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Yesterday wet, today dry, source still unknown

Client Elena reported water near air handler AH-6 at 15:00 yesterday. Today's visit note states that the nearby floor is dry and the source was not identified. An earlier service log mentions a drain-pan concern but does not establish a link to the reported water. Coordinator Kai must preserve the chronology, distinguish each source, and summarize the unresolved issue. The exercise supplies records only; no equipment inspection or diagnostic procedure is requested.

Elena: Client / Kai: Service coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What did Elena report yesterday?

Answer and explanation

D. Water near AH-6 at 15:00

Elena's account concerns observed water near AH-6 at the stated time.

2. What does today's visit note establish?

Answer and explanation

D. The nearby floor was dry and the source was not identified

The note records a later surface condition and an unresolved source.

3. What does the earlier drain-pan entry prove?

Answer and explanation

A. Only that a concern was recorded, not a causal link

The historical concern is not linked to the water by the supplied evidence.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Yesterday at 15:00, Elena reported water near AH-6. Today, the floor is recorded as dry. Those observations concern different times; the later note does not disprove the earlier report or establish a repair.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
condensate
Liquid formed when water vapor condenses.identify a condensate reference
drain pan
Component intended to collect relevant drainage or condensate in equipment.record a drain-pan concern
condensate line
Pipe or tube associated with carrying condensate away.identify the condensate line
moisture report
Account describing water or dampness at a location.retain the moisture report
service log
Chronological record of service-related information.consult the service log
visit note
Record of observations or actions during an attendance.quote the visit note
historical entry
Record created before the current event.distinguish a historical entry
chronology
Ordered sequence of events and observations.preserve the chronology
reported water
Water described in an account before its cause is established.locate the reported water
nearby floor
Floor surface close to the referenced equipment.describe the nearby floor
source identification
Establishing where the reported water originated.keep source identification open
causal link
Supported connection between a condition and its cause.establish a causal link
unresolved concern
Issue that has not been confirmed as corrected or closed.retain an unresolved concern
dry observation
Record that the visible surface was dry at the stated time.qualify the dry observation
intermittent symptom
Condition that may appear at some times and not others.describe an intermittent symptom
recurrence history
Record of repeated appearances of a condition.verify recurrence history
drainage path
Route intended for the relevant liquid to travel.identify the drainage path
overflow
Liquid exceeding the capacity or boundary of its intended containment.distinguish a reported overflow
leakage
Unintended escape of a fluid.avoid assuming leakage source
condensation
Formation of liquid from vapor under relevant conditions.distinguish condensation from a diagnosis
finding
Observation or conclusion established through the relevant assessment.state the actual finding
repair status
Whether corrective work is proposed, underway, or completed.verify repair status
evidence boundary
Limit of what the information supports.preserve the evidence boundary
record reconciliation
Bringing separate accounts together without erasing their distinctions.complete record reconciliation

The earlier drain-pan concern is history, not a verified cause. Do not change near AH-6 to from AH-6, or concern to failure, without evidence. The source remains unidentified in the supplied visit note.

C Notice the language

Keep two times visible in one account

A later observation need not contradict an earlier report. Use time markers and source attribution rather than choosing one account and deleting the other. A prior concern can remain relevant history without becoming the confirmed cause of a new symptom.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State yesterday's account
The client reported water near AH-6 at 15:00 yesterday.
State today's observation
The visit note records the nearby floor as dry today.
Keep both times
Those accounts refer to different observations at different times.
Name the unresolved issue
The source was not identified.
Attribute the history
An earlier service-log entry mentions a drain-pan concern.
Avoid converting history into cause
That entry does not establish a link to yesterday's water.
Distinguish near from from
Near AH-6 gives a location, not a proven origin.
Avoid changing concern to failure
The record says concern, not confirmed failure.
Keep repair separate
No completed repair is established by the dry-floor note.
Preserve the client report
The later observation does not cancel your account.
Use the exact equipment reference
Please retain AH-6 in the summary.
Avoid an invented recurrence pattern
We have not established how often this happens.
Name the three sources
Client report, visit note, and earlier log remain distinct.
Limit the finding
We can report the surface condition without diagnosing the source.
Give a balanced summary
Water reported yesterday; floor dry today; cause unresolved.
Close with accurate status
The source question remains open for the relevant assessment.
6 language and precision notes
Yesterday versus today
Prevents observations at different times from being treated as a contradiction.
Near versus from
Distinguishes location from origin.
Mentions
Records what a document says without upgrading it to a verified diagnosis.
Does not establish a link
Keeps a plausible history item from becoming a proven cause.
Recorded as
Attributes an observation to the supplied note.
Remains open
Preserves an unresolved matter without inventing an outcome.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which summary preserves chronology?

Answer and explanation

D. Water near AH-6 yesterday at 15:00; floor dry today; source unidentified

The accurate summary retains near AH-6, yesterday's 15:00 report, today's dry floor, and the unidentified source without changing attribution or inventing cause.

2. Which wording improperly asserts origin?

Answer and explanation

C. Water came from AH-6

The supplied report gives proximity, not proof that AH-6 produced the water.

3. How should the drain-pan entry be handled?

Answer and explanation

C. Retain it as history with no established causal link

Relevant history can be preserved without asserting a cause or resolution.

4. What can the dry-floor note establish about repair?

Answer and explanation

B. No completed repair by itself

A surface observation is not evidence that corrective work occurred.

Yesterday wet, today dry, source still unknown

Elena: Client / Kai: Service coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

  1. Elena
    Kai, yesterday I reported water near AH-6 at three. Today's note says dry floor. I don't want my report dropped because the visit happened later.
  2. Kai
    We can preserve the 1. Your account and today's note refer to different times, so a dry floor now does not cancel the water you reported yesterday.
  3. Elena
    Please keep yesterday and fifteen hundred attached to my account. Otherwise it could sound as though the technician saw water on today's visit.
  4. Kai
    I will. The 2 records today's nearby floor as dry and says the source was not identified. That is a separate observation with its own limits.
  5. Elena
    The earlier service log mentions a drain-pan concern. Someone suggested putting that as the cause so the record has an explanation.
  6. Kai
    We should retain the 3 as history, but the entry does not establish a connection to yesterday's report. It is not enough to name the pan as the proven source.
  7. Elena
    I only know the water was near the air handler. Writing that it came from AH-6 would be more specific than what I observed.
  8. Kai
    Exactly. That is an 4. Changing near AH-6 to from AH-6 would turn a location description into an unsupported statement about origin.
  9. Elena
    The old wording is concern, not failure. Could you retain that distinction rather than turning a historical note into a confirmed broken part?
  10. Kai
    It would change it. The 5 says concern, not confirmed failure. We need to preserve that distinction even if the topic appears relevant to the current question.
  11. Elena
    Then we'll have an unresolved source, but an accurate record. I don't want a neat explanation that neither the client report nor visit note establishes.
  12. Kai
    Then the 6 stays open. We can report the facts and the earlier concern without claiming that the relationship between them has been verified.
  13. Elena
    Does dry today imply someone repaired it overnight? I haven't supplied any record of work between my report and the visit.
  14. Kai
    No. The 7 cannot be inferred from the dry-floor observation. Nothing in these records establishes that a repair was completed between your report and the visit.
  15. Elena
    One older concern also doesn't establish a pattern of repeated water events. We shouldn't attach a frequency to the problem without the history.
  16. Kai
    Correct. A 8 would need supporting information. We should not invent a frequency or treat every earlier note as another occurrence of the same water event.
  17. Elena
    Please combine the three records without losing their sources: my account, today's visit note, and the earlier drain-pan entry.
  18. Kai
    Client reported water near AH-6 at 15:00 yesterday. Today's floor was dry; source unidentified. Earlier log notes a drain-pan concern, with no established 9 to the water.
  19. Elena
    That's the chronology I need. It neither dismisses yesterday's observation nor treats today's dry floor as proof that the source has been fixed.
  20. Kai
    I will use that 10 in the handoff. The next assessment can address the source question with the original accounts intact, rather than starting from an assumed diagnosis.

Answers and explanations
  1. chronology Turn 2. Chronology keeps yesterday's report and today's observation separate instead of treating them as simultaneous claims.
  2. visit note Turn 4. Visit note is the source for today's dry-floor observation, not yesterday's client report.
  3. drain-pan concern Turn 6. Drain-pan concern is historical information, not a confirmed cause of the later water report.
  4. evidence boundary Turn 8. Evidence boundary limits the account to observed location rather than asserting origin from AH-6.
  5. historical entry Turn 10. Historical entry must retain its recorded status as a concern rather than being upgraded to confirmed failure.
  6. source identification Turn 12. Source identification remains unresolved because today's note explicitly says the source was not identified.
  7. repair status Turn 14. Repair status cannot be inferred from a later dry surface when no corrective work is documented.
  8. recurrence history Turn 16. Recurrence history requires evidence of repeated events, not merely an earlier concern about a component.
  9. causal link Turn 18. Causal link is the missing connection between the earlier concern and the reported water.
  10. record reconciliation Turn 20. Record reconciliation combines the three sources while preserving time, attribution, and uncertainty.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Elena: Kai, yesterday I reported water near AH-6 at three. Today's note says dry floor. I don't want my report dropped because the visit happened later.
  2. Kai: We can preserve the chronology. Your account and today's note refer to different times, so a dry floor now does not cancel the water you reported yesterday.
  3. Elena: Please keep yesterday and fifteen hundred attached to my account. Otherwise it could sound as though the technician saw water on today's visit.
  4. Kai: I will. The visit note records today's nearby floor as dry and says the source was not identified. That is a separate observation with its own limits.
  5. Elena: The earlier service log mentions a drain-pan concern. Someone suggested putting that as the cause so the record has an explanation.
  6. Kai: We should retain the drain-pan concern as history, but the entry does not establish a connection to yesterday's report. It is not enough to name the pan as the proven source.
  7. Elena: I only know the water was near the air handler. Writing that it came from AH-6 would be more specific than what I observed.
  8. Kai: Exactly. That is an evidence boundary. Changing near AH-6 to from AH-6 would turn a location description into an unsupported statement about origin.
  9. Elena: The old wording is concern, not failure. Could you retain that distinction rather than turning a historical note into a confirmed broken part?
  10. Kai: It would change it. The historical entry says concern, not confirmed failure. We need to preserve that distinction even if the topic appears relevant to the current question.
  11. Elena: Then we'll have an unresolved source, but an accurate record. I don't want a neat explanation that neither the client report nor visit note establishes.
  12. Kai: Then the source identification stays open. We can report the facts and the earlier concern without claiming that the relationship between them has been verified.
  13. Elena: Does dry today imply someone repaired it overnight? I haven't supplied any record of work between my report and the visit.
  14. Kai: No. The repair status cannot be inferred from the dry-floor observation. Nothing in these records establishes that a repair was completed between your report and the visit.
  15. Elena: One older concern also doesn't establish a pattern of repeated water events. We shouldn't attach a frequency to the problem without the history.
  16. Kai: Correct. A recurrence history would need supporting information. We should not invent a frequency or treat every earlier note as another occurrence of the same water event.
  17. Elena: Please combine the three records without losing their sources: my account, today's visit note, and the earlier drain-pan entry.
  18. Kai: Client reported water near AH-6 at 15:00 yesterday. Today's floor was dry; source unidentified. Earlier log notes a drain-pan concern, with no established causal link to the water.
  19. Elena: That's the chronology I need. It neither dismisses yesterday's observation nor treats today's dry floor as proof that the source has been fixed.
  20. Kai: I will use that record reconciliation in the handoff. The next assessment can address the source question with the original accounts intact, rather than starting from an assumed diagnosis.
