Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Yesterday's water and today's dry floor

A technician explains the supplied AH-6 notes to the client.

  1. Technician: Today's note records a dry floor near AH-6, not a confirmed resolution of yesterday's report.
  2. Client: I noticed water there at three. The old service log mentions the drain pan.
  3. Technician: That earlier entry is relevant history, but it doesn't establish the source of this report.
  4. Client: Could it have been condensate from the equipment, rather than another source of water?
  5. Technician: That's a possibility for technical assessment, not a confirmed diagnosis from these notes.
  6. Client: Please link the older entry so the reviewer knows the pan was discussed before.
  7. Technician: I'll retain the dates and describe any suspected explanation as a provisional diagnosis.
  8. Client: Does a dry floor mean we should mark the service request resolved for the owner?
  9. Technician: Not on this observation alone. The source question remains open for the reviewer.
  10. Client: I'll tell the owner what was reported, what today's note says, and what remains unknown.
An old repair appears under the wrong tag

A technician queries a service-log entry with a coordinator.

  1. Technician: The drain-pan entry is filed under AH-7, but its attached photograph is labeled AH-8.
  2. Coordinator: Those units sit beside each other. The technician may have selected the wrong tag.
  3. Technician: We need the author to verify the reference rather than deciding from that possibility.
  4. Coordinator: I'll retain both labels and request clarification from the original visit record.
  5. Technician: The client has asked whether AH-7 has had three repeated pan-related visits.
  6. Coordinator: We shouldn't count this entry toward that history until the equipment link is confirmed.
  7. Technician: Exactly. An uncertain filing reference could create a misleading pattern of repeat faults.
  8. Coordinator: I'll distinguish the two confirmed AH-7 entries from this third unresolved record.
  9. Technician: Please include that limitation when asking the reviewer about the current reported moisture.
  10. Coordinator: I will send the records with their actual status, not a simplified three-fault claim.
Noise reported only during the morning meeting

A technician gathers an existing noise account from a workplace manager.

  1. Technician: The request says constant rattling. When have people actually noticed the sound?
  2. Workplace manager: They mentioned it during Monday's morning meeting and again at Tuesday's briefing.
  3. Technician: Then constant may overstate the report. We have two reported periods, not continuous observation.
  4. Workplace manager: You're right. I used constant because the complaints kept coming to my inbox.
  5. Technician: Let's preserve the repeated reports but identify their times and the room.
  6. Workplace manager: Both concern Meeting Room C, near the ceiling opening closest to the screen.
  7. Technician: I'll record that location without identifying a fan or duct as the confirmed source.
  8. Workplace manager: Should I ask the team to change the controls to see whether the sound returns?
  9. Technician: No reproduction is needed for this language task. Their existing accounts are sufficient to relay.
  10. Workplace manager: I'll correct the description to recurring reported noise and arrange the technical assessment separately.

Lesson 05: Reporting moisture, symptoms, and service findings
Original fictional case: Client Elena reported water near air handler AH-6 at 15:00 yesterday. Today's visit note states that the nearby floor is dry and the source was not identified. An earlier service log mentions a drain-pan concern but does not establish a link to the reported water. Coordinator Kai must preserve the chronology, distinguish each source, and summarize the unresolved issue. The exercise supplies records only; no equipment inspection or diagnostic procedure is requested.
Language workshop: Yesterday wet, today dry, source still unknown
Communication goal: Reconcile a historical report with a later observation and avoid treating an earlier maintenance concern as a proven cause.
Language guidance: Yesterday at 15:00, Elena reported water near AH-6. Today, the floor is recorded as dry. Those observations concern different times; the later note does not disprove the earlier report or establish a repair.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The client reported water near AH-6 at 15:00 yesterday. / The visit note records the nearby floor as dry today. / Those accounts refer to different observations at different times. / The source was not identified. / An earlier service-log entry mentions a drain-pan concern. / That entry does not establish a link to yesterday's water. / Near AH-6 gives a location, not a proven origin. / The record says concern, not confirmed failure. / No completed repair is established by the dry-floor note. / The later observation does not cancel your account. / Please retain AH-6 in the summary. / We have not established how often this happens. / Client report, visit note, and earlier log remain distinct. / We can report the surface condition without diagnosing the source. / Water reported yesterday; floor dry today; cause unresolved. / The source question remains open for the relevant assessment.
Vocabulary: condensate: Liquid formed when water vapor condenses. / drain pan: Component intended to collect relevant drainage or condensate in equipment. / condensate line: Pipe or tube associated with carrying condensate away. / moisture report: Account describing water or dampness at a location. / service log: Chronological record of service-related information. / visit note: Record of observations or actions during an attendance. / historical entry: Record created before the current event. / chronology: Ordered sequence of events and observations. / reported water: Water described in an account before its cause is established. / nearby floor: Floor surface close to the referenced equipment. / source identification: Establishing where the reported water originated. / causal link: Supported connection between a condition and its cause. / unresolved concern: Issue that has not been confirmed as corrected or closed. / dry observation: Record that the visible surface was dry at the stated time. / intermittent symptom: Condition that may appear at some times and not others. / recurrence history: Record of repeated appearances of a condition. / drainage path: Route intended for the relevant liquid to travel. / overflow: Liquid exceeding the capacity or boundary of its intended containment. / leakage: Unintended escape of a fluid. / condensation: Formation of liquid from vapor under relevant conditions. / finding: Observation or conclusion established through the relevant assessment. / repair status: Whether corrective work is proposed, underway, or completed. / evidence boundary: Limit of what the information supports. / record reconciliation: Bringing separate accounts together without erasing their distinctions.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Stress yesterday at fifteen hundred, today dry, and earlier concern as three separate records. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep near distinct from from, and dry distinct from repaired. Complete and check the summary. Read the source status without treating historical concern as a confirmed cause.
Partner: Elena: Client / Kai: Service coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the service summary. Keep yesterday, today, and the earlier concern distinct.

06Discussing refrigeration alarms and temperature recordsAn alarm, a display value, and missing product data+

Your goal

Report an alarm chronology accurately, distinguish cabinet and product information, and route a food-safety question to the responsible lead.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

An alarm, a display value, and missing product data

Cafe manager Noah discusses a refrigeration record with technician Imani. A cabinet display recorded 8 degrees Celsius at 06:10. Product temperatures are not supplied. An alarm-history entry and a scheduled-defrost entry have the same time, but event cause and duration are unconfirmed. Noah has already alerted the responsible food-safety lead under the site procedures; this discussion occurs alongside that escalation and does not delay protective action. He asks how the records relate to the food-safety question. The display value and coincident entries do not authorize food release or establish a harmless defrost event.

Noah: Cafe manager / Imani: Refrigeration technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What was recorded at 06:10?

Answer and explanation

C. A cabinet display value of 8 degrees Celsius

The record supplies a cabinet display value, not product temperatures or duration.

2. What do the matching timestamps establish?

Answer and explanation

B. The two entries share a recorded time

Coincident timestamps do not establish cause, duration, or product safety.

3. Who must decide the food-safety question?

Answer and explanation

A. The responsible food-safety lead under site procedures

The scenario assigns that decision to the responsible lead using the site's procedures.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The record gives a display value of 8 degrees Celsius at 06:10. Product temperatures are absent. No safe holding period or release decision can be inferred from this isolated display value and timestamp.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
refrigerated cabinet
Enclosed equipment used to hold products under cooled conditions.identify the refrigerated cabinet
cabinet display
Interface showing a value or status from refrigeration equipment.quote the cabinet display
product temperature
Temperature of the actual stored product.distinguish product temperature
display value
Number shown by the equipment interface.record the display value
alarm history
Log of recorded equipment alarm events.review the alarm history
timestamp
Recorded date or time associated with an entry.preserve the timestamp
defrost
Process intended to remove frost from relevant equipment surfaces.identify the defrost entry
scheduled defrost
Defrost event listed in the control schedule or record.distinguish scheduled defrost
event duration
Length of time an event continued.establish event duration
coincident entries
Records appearing at the same stated time.compare coincident entries
causation
Relationship in which one event produces another.avoid assuming causation
temperature excursion
Departure from a defined temperature range or limit.assess a recorded temperature excursion
exposure history
Record of conditions experienced by the product over time.establish exposure history
data gap
Missing information needed for an assessment.identify the data gap
food-safety lead
Person assigned responsibility for the relevant food-safety decision.notify the food-safety lead
site procedure
Established process applicable at the premises.follow the site procedure
release decision
Authorization to make a product available for its intended use.refer the release decision
product disposition
Decision about how affected products will be handled.determine product disposition
alarm acknowledgment
Record that an alarm has been noticed or accepted by an operator.distinguish alarm acknowledgment
alarm clearance
Change in alarm status that does not alone establish product safety.distinguish alarm clearance
trend record
Series of values showing change over time.review the trend record
sensor location
Position associated with a particular measurement device.identify the sensor location
technical cause
Established equipment or process explanation for an event.keep the technical cause open
escalation summary
Concise information passed to the responsible decision-maker.give an escalation summary

The alarm and scheduled-defrost entries coincide, but cause and duration remain unconfirmed. Do not call the event harmless or assume defrost explains it. Follow the site food-safety escalation process; the responsible lead decides product disposition.

C Notice the language

Give the record, not an improvised release decision

Operational pressure can turn a request for information into a request for reassurance. Identify exactly what the record contains and what is missing. Refer the product decision to the responsible food-safety lead while preserving the technical event as unresolved.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Quote the actual record
The cabinet display recorded 8 degrees Celsius at 06:10.
Name the missing information
Product temperatures have not been supplied.
Keep the record type clear
That is a display value, not a verified food-temperature record.
Identify the coincident entries
The alarm and scheduled-defrost entries share a timestamp.
Avoid a causal shortcut
The matching time does not establish that defrost caused the alarm.
Preserve duration uncertainty
The event duration is unconfirmed.
Reject premature reassurance
I cannot declare the food safe from these records.
Name the decision route
The responsible food-safety lead must decide under the site procedures.
Keep technical status open
The cause of the event still needs confirmation.
Avoid equating clearance with safety
Alarm clearance would not itself release the food.
Preserve the data gap
The product exposure history is not established.
Escalate accurately
Please pass the display record and the missing data to the lead.
Keep disposition separate
Product disposition is separate from explaining the equipment entry.
Avoid a guessed threshold
We should not invent an allowable exposure period.
Read back the essentials
8 degrees at 06:10; product data absent; cause and duration unconfirmed.
Close without releasing product
This technical discussion does not authorize food release.
6 language and precision notes
Recorded
Attributes the number to the equipment record without changing what it measures.
Same time versus because
Separates coincidence from a causal explanation.
Unconfirmed duration
Prevents a timestamp from being read as a complete event interval.
Must decide under
Names both the responsible role and the governing site process.
Would not itself
Limits what an alarm-status change can establish.
Separate from
Keeps equipment assessment and product disposition as distinct questions.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which summary belongs in the escalation?

Answer and explanation

C. Display 8 degrees Celsius at 06:10; product data absent; cause and duration unconfirmed

The summary preserves the actual value and all material information limits.

2. Why is defrost caused it unsupported?

Answer and explanation

D. The shared timestamp does not establish causation

Coincident entries show a shared recorded time, not a verified cause, completed cycle, duration, or food-temperature history.

3. Who should receive the food-release question?

Answer and explanation

D. The responsible food-safety lead using site procedures

Product decisions require the assigned responsible process rather than an improvised technical assurance.

4. Which statement improperly fills a data gap?

Answer and explanation

C. The food was warm for only one minute

No event duration or product exposure period is established by the record.

E Conversations

An alarm, a display value, and missing product data

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Noah: Cafe manager / Imani: Refrigeration technician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • scheduled defrost
  • timestamp
  • Product disposition
  • exposure history
  • Alarm clearance
  • event duration
  • product temperature
  • display value
  • escalation summary
  • food-safety lead
  1. Noah
    Imani, we've alerted our food-safety lead and aren't treating this call as clearance to serve the affected food. Can you help explain the cabinet record?
  2. Imani
    Yes. The 1 is eight degrees Celsius at 06:10. It describes the equipment record, not a verified temperature for every stored item.
  3. Noah
    That's all I've been given for temperature: the cabinet display. There are no food readings in the information I'm sending to the lead.
  4. Imani
    Then 2 is missing. Keep that gap explicit; don't copy the display number into a field that would imply the actual food was measured.
  5. Noah
    The alarm history and the scheduled-defrost entry both say 06:10. Could I describe the alarm as caused by defrost?
  6. Imani
    The shared 3 only tells us the entries coincide. It doesn't establish the cause, prove the event was harmless, or determine what happened to the products.
  7. Noah
    The owner asked how long it lasted. There's only that one recorded time in this extract, with no confirmed ending time.
  8. Imani
    Then 4 is unconfirmed. A timestamp doesn't mean a one-minute event, and we shouldn't invent an interval to make the report look complete.
  9. Noah
    I'll keep the lead updated with those gaps. Our opening time is close, but the technical call isn't a substitute for the product decision.
  10. Imani
    Correct. The 5 must decide under your site procedures. Follow that process while the equipment information is clarified; don't wait for a reassuring guess from the display.
  11. Noah
    Please distinguish the scheduled item from the actual explanation. I want the handoff to say what the log contains without implying we've diagnosed the event.
  12. Imani
    It contains a 6 entry at the same recorded time as the alarm. That association needs assessment; it isn't yet a confirmed causal explanation.
  13. Noah
    Suppose a later update says the alarm has disappeared. Would that alone answer whether the affected stock can be used?
  14. Imani
    No. 7 describes alarm status, not the products' earlier conditions. It doesn't replace the responsible food-safety decision or establish an acceptable exposure history.
  15. Noah
    So I should leave the earlier product conditions unresolved, rather than assume that a cleared display would make the whole episode irrelevant.
  16. Imani
    Exactly. The 8 isn't established by this extract. Keep the missing product data, duration, and cause visible alongside the known display reading.
  17. Noah
    My message says display eight Celsius at 06:10; no product readings supplied; alarm and defrost entries coincide; duration and cause unconfirmed.
  18. Imani
    That's a useful 9. Send any relevant updates through the site process as they become available, keeping the product question separate from the equipment diagnosis.
  19. Noah
    The lead will handle the food decision under our procedures. I'll preserve the technical record and won't describe this call as approval to resume using the stock.
  20. Imani
    Right. 10 belongs to that responsible process. We have clarified the supplied evidence, not declared the food safe or resolved the equipment event.

Answers and explanations
  1. display value Turn 2. Display value is equipment information and does not supply the missing product temperatures or exposure history.
  2. product temperature Turn 4. Product temperature concerns the food itself and is absent from the information supplied.
  3. timestamp Turn 6. Timestamp establishes the recorded time shared by the entries, not why the alarm occurred.
  4. event duration Turn 8. Event duration remains unknown because one timestamp does not establish a complete interval.
  5. food-safety lead Turn 10. Food-safety lead is the assigned decision-maker for product safety under the site's procedures.
  6. scheduled defrost Turn 12. Scheduled defrost is a recorded event sharing the time, not a proven explanation for the alarm.
  7. Alarm clearance Turn 14. Alarm clearance concerns equipment alarm status and does not establish product safety or past exposure.
  8. exposure history Turn 16. Exposure history describes conditions experienced by the products over time, which these isolated records do not establish.
  9. escalation summary Turn 18. Escalation summary passes the actual records and missing information to the responsible lead without authorizing release.
  10. Product disposition Turn 20. Product disposition is the responsible decision about the food, distinct from interpreting the equipment records.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The cabinet reading is not the product reading

A refrigeration technician discusses the fictional cafe log with its manager.

  1. Technician: The cabinet temperature display logged eight degrees Celsius at six ten, not a product temperature.
  2. Cafe manager: Can you tell me from that whether the stored food is safe to sell?
  3. Technician: No. We have no supplied product-temperature result, and that decision needs your food-safety lead.
  4. Cafe manager: The alarm and scheduled defrost cycle have the same timestamp. Doesn't that explain it?
  5. Technician: The coincidence is relevant, but it doesn't establish cause, duration, or product condition.
  6. Cafe manager: I'll involve the food-safety lead now rather than wait for an equipment explanation.
  7. Technician: Please retain the alarm history and identify which cabinet and display produced this value.
  8. Cafe manager: It's cabinet FR-3. I'll send the existing record with its timestamp and units.
  9. Technician: We'll ask the technical reviewer to clarify the equipment event without treating that as stock clearance.
  10. Cafe manager: Understood. Equipment assessment and the product decision will remain separate, with both concerns followed up.
Two clocks disagree

A technician clarifies refrigeration event times with a shop supervisor.

  1. Technician: The cabinet alarm says six ten, while the staff log records the concern at seven ten.
  2. Shop supervisor: I assumed there were two separate events, exactly an hour apart from each other.
  3. Technician: Do both records use the same time basis? The equipment clock setting isn't identified here.
  4. Shop supervisor: I don't know. The staff log uses the wall clock in the office.
  5. Technician: Then we should verify the timestamps before describing a repeated event or its duration.
  6. Shop supervisor: I'll ask the service coordinator to confirm the record's time basis, without changing either original entry.
  7. Technician: Please preserve the recorded values and identify any later correction as a correction.
  8. Shop supervisor: The stock lead also needs the timeline. Should I give them only the earlier time?
  9. Technician: Give both sources with the unresolved difference; don't choose one as confirmed without evidence.
  10. Shop supervisor: I'll send that qualified timeline so they know exactly what remains uncertain.
An acknowledged alarm is not a resolved condition

A technician explains an alarm-history label to a facilities client.

  1. Technician: The history marks the alarm acknowledged, but it doesn't show a confirmed resolution.
  2. Facilities client: I thought acknowledged meant the cause had been corrected and the cabinet was fine.
  3. Technician: It records recognition of the alarm, not necessarily resolution of the underlying condition.
  4. Facilities client: Then our report should not say fault cleared based on that status alone.
  5. Technician: Correct. We need the actual event history and technical findings to establish the outcome.
  6. Facilities client: The food-safety lead asked whether an acknowledgment clears the product for use.
  7. Technician: That status cannot establish product suitability. They need the relevant product information and site procedures.
  8. Facilities client: I'll preserve acknowledged as the equipment-log status, with the condition and stock decisions separate.
  9. Technician: Please include the event identifier so the reviewer can locate the specific alarm.
  10. Facilities client: I will ask for its verified outcome rather than infer it from the acknowledgment label.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

An alarm, a display value, and missing product data
  1. Read turns 1-10. Keep the immediate site escalation in place while separating display temperature, product data, and event duration.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Contrast a scheduled-defrost entry with established cause, and alarm clearance with product disposition.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Escalate the record without a safety claim

Complete the handoff to the responsible lead. Preserve the display record and the missing product information.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: "The display recorded ___ degrees Celsius."

Answer and explanation

A. 8

Eight is the cabinet display value, not a verified product temperature.

2. Technician: "The timestamp was ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 06:10

The timestamp records one time and does not establish event duration.

3. Manager: "Product temperatures were not ___."

Answer and explanation

D. supplied

The absence of product readings is a material limit on the information.

4. Technician: "The responsible food-safety ___ must decide under site procedures."

Answer and explanation

C. lead

The assigned lead makes the product decision rather than the equipment display alone.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 06: Discussing refrigeration alarms and temperature records
Original fictional case: Cafe manager Noah discusses a refrigeration record with technician Imani. A cabinet display recorded 8 degrees Celsius at 06:10. Product temperatures are not supplied. An alarm-history entry and a scheduled-defrost entry have the same time, but event cause and duration are unconfirmed. Noah has already alerted the responsible food-safety lead under the site procedures; this discussion occurs alongside that escalation and does not delay protective action. He asks how the records relate to the food-safety question. The display value and coincident entries do not authorize food release or establish a harmless defrost event.
Language workshop: An alarm, a display value, and missing product data
Communication goal: Report an alarm chronology accurately, distinguish cabinet and product information, and route a food-safety question to the responsible lead.
Language guidance: The record gives a display value of 8 degrees Celsius at 06:10. Product temperatures are absent. No safe holding period or release decision can be inferred from this isolated display value and timestamp.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The cabinet display recorded 8 degrees Celsius at 06:10. / Product temperatures have not been supplied. / That is a display value, not a verified food-temperature record. / The alarm and scheduled-defrost entries share a timestamp. / The matching time does not establish that defrost caused the alarm. / The event duration is unconfirmed. / I cannot declare the food safe from these records. / The responsible food-safety lead must decide under the site procedures. / The cause of the event still needs confirmation. / Alarm clearance would not itself release the food. / The product exposure history is not established. / Please pass the display record and the missing data to the lead. / Product disposition is separate from explaining the equipment entry. / We should not invent an allowable exposure period. / 8 degrees at 06:10; product data absent; cause and duration unconfirmed. / This technical discussion does not authorize food release.
Vocabulary: refrigerated cabinet: Enclosed equipment used to hold products under cooled conditions. / cabinet display: Interface showing a value or status from refrigeration equipment. / product temperature: Temperature of the actual stored product. / display value: Number shown by the equipment interface. / alarm history: Log of recorded equipment alarm events. / timestamp: Recorded date or time associated with an entry. / defrost: Process intended to remove frost from relevant equipment surfaces. / scheduled defrost: Defrost event listed in the control schedule or record. / event duration: Length of time an event continued. / coincident entries: Records appearing at the same stated time. / causation: Relationship in which one event produces another. / temperature excursion: Departure from a defined temperature range or limit. / exposure history: Record of conditions experienced by the product over time. / data gap: Missing information needed for an assessment. / food-safety lead: Person assigned responsibility for the relevant food-safety decision. / site procedure: Established process applicable at the premises. / release decision: Authorization to make a product available for its intended use. / product disposition: Decision about how affected products will be handled. / alarm acknowledgment: Record that an alarm has been noticed or accepted by an operator. / alarm clearance: Change in alarm status that does not alone establish product safety. / trend record: Series of values showing change over time. / sensor location: Position associated with a particular measurement device. / technical cause: Established equipment or process explanation for an event. / escalation summary: Concise information passed to the responsible decision-maker.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Keep the immediate site escalation in place while separating display temperature, product data, and event duration. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Contrast a scheduled-defrost entry with established cause, and alarm clearance with product disposition. Complete and check the handoff. Preserve the missing data and responsible lead; do not add a food-release claim.
Partner: Noah: Cafe manager / Imani: Refrigeration technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the handoff to the responsible lead. Preserve the display record and the missing product information.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Noah: My message says display eight Celsius at 06:10; no product readings supplied; alarm and defrost entries coincide; duration and cause unconfirmed.
Imani: That's a useful escalation summary. Send any relevant updates through the site process as they become available, keeping the product question separate from the equipment diagnosis.
Noah: The lead will handle the food decision under our procedures. I'll preserve the technical record and won't describe this call as approval to resume using the stock.
Imani: Right. Product disposition belongs to that responsible process. We have clarified the supplied evidence, not declared the food safe or resolved the equipment event.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Coordinating ductwork, access, and changesMoving a grille is a coordinated change+

Your goal

Acknowledge an appearance preference, explain unassessed consequences, and present two bounded choices without promising price or timing.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Moving a grille is a coordinated change

Office client Priya asks project coordinator Daniel to move a supply grille because it visually conflicts with a pendant light. The proposal has not assessed ductwork, ceiling restoration, service access, price, or timing. Priya may retain the current reviewed layout or request a coordinated change proposal. Daniel must explain the choice without treating a small visible move as a minor technical task or guaranteeing unchanged cost and completion.

Priya: Office client / Daniel: Project coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Why does Priya want the grille moved?

Answer and explanation

D. It visually conflicts with a pendant light

The request concerns appearance, not an established system fault.

2. Which consequences have been assessed?

Answer and explanation

A. None of the listed change effects

Ductwork, restoration, access, price, and timing remain unassessed.

3. What are the stated choices?

Answer and explanation

C. Retain the reviewed layout or request a coordinated change proposal

The brief supplies two bounded options without authorizing an immediate alteration.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The visible conflict concerns the grille and pendant light. No performance fault is established. Ductwork, restoration, access, price, and timing all need assessment before the proposed move can be treated as a defined change.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
supply grille relocation
Proposed change to the position of a supply-air grille.assess a supply grille relocation
pendant light
Light fitting suspended from the ceiling.coordinate the pendant light
visual conflict
Appearance issue between elements in the same view.describe the visual conflict
reflected ceiling plan
Drawing of ceiling elements represented as viewed in reflection from below.review the reflected ceiling plan
ceiling void
Space above the visible ceiling and below the structure above.coordinate the ceiling void
branch duct
Duct section branching from a larger distribution route.review the branch duct
duct connection
Interface joining a duct to another component.assess the duct connection
flexible duct
Air-conveying duct with a flexible construction.identify the flexible duct
rigid duct
Air-conveying duct with a relatively fixed form.distinguish rigid duct
service clearance
Space required for relevant maintenance or access.preserve service clearance
ceiling restoration
Reinstatement of ceiling finishes after defined work.assess ceiling restoration
access provision
Means included in the design for reaching services.review the access provision
reviewed layout
Arrangement that has undergone the stated project review.retain the reviewed layout
change proposal
Defined suggestion for altering the existing arrangement.request a change proposal
coordination review
Evaluation of the change across affected elements and trades.arrange a coordination review
scope impact
Effect of a change on the work required.identify the scope impact
cost impact
Effect of a change on price or expenditure.assess the cost impact
schedule impact
Effect of a change on timing.assess the schedule impact
design preference
Client choice concerning the desired arrangement or appearance.record the design preference
technical feasibility
Whether the proposed arrangement can satisfy relevant technical conditions.establish technical feasibility
revised layout
Updated arrangement proposed or accepted after a change.review the revised layout
unassessed consequence
Effect not yet evaluated.keep unassessed consequences visible
instruction to proceed
Direction authorizing agreed work to begin.distinguish an instruction to proceed
decision record
Record of the option selected and its conditions.confirm the decision record

The two available decisions are to retain the reviewed layout or request a coordinated proposal. Choosing assessment does not approve a new location, authorize installation, or guarantee that the original cost and schedule will remain unchanged.

C Notice the language

Make the choice clear without minimizing the work

A visible component can be connected to less visible work above the ceiling. Acknowledge the design preference, list the specific unassessed matters, and offer the stated choices. A request to assess a move is not approval to install it or an agreement to absorb its cost.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the preference
I understand why you want the grille clear of the pendant light.
Name the request
You are asking to relocate the supply grille.
Avoid minimizing hidden work
The visible move may involve work above the ceiling.
Identify the unassessed items
Ductwork, restoration, access, price, and timing still need assessment.
Separate appearance and fault
The concern is visual, not a confirmed performance defect.
State the first option
You can retain the current reviewed layout.
State the second option
You can request a coordinated change proposal.
Keep the choice bounded
Those are the two decisions available at this stage.
Avoid a price promise
I cannot confirm unchanged cost before review.
Avoid a timing promise
The schedule effect has not been established.
Keep access in scope
The proposal must account for service access.
Clarify the request status
Asking for a proposal does not authorize the relocation.
Preserve technical review
The new position needs the relevant coordination.
Name restoration explicitly
Ceiling restoration must be included in the assessment.
Read back the decision
You are requesting assessment, not approving installation.
Close with the next deliverable
We will bring back a defined proposal with its consequences.
6 language and precision notes
May involve
Acknowledges possible hidden work without inventing a specific construction requirement.
Still need assessment
Keeps every listed consequence open.
Retain or request
Presents two concrete choices instead of an unbounded design task.
Before review
Makes evidence a condition of a price or timing commitment.
Not a confirmed defect
Distinguishes an appearance preference from a performance finding.
With its consequences
Requires the proposal to show related effects, not merely a new dot on a plan.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response explains the choice accurately?

Answer and explanation

C. Retain the reviewed layout or request a coordinated proposal with impacts assessed

The response offers the two supplied options and preserves the need to assess consequences.

2. Which item must not disappear from review?

Answer and explanation

B. Service access

Service access is explicitly one of the unassessed matters in the brief.

3. What does requesting a proposal authorize here?

Answer and explanation

A. Assessment of the change, not its installation

A request for review does not equal agreement to carry out the proposed work.

4. Which phrase overstates certainty?

Answer and explanation

D. We can move it with no cost or timing effect

Cost and timing effects remain unknown, so a no-impact promise is unsupported.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

Moving a grille is a coordinated change

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Priya: Office client / Daniel: Project coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • visual conflict
  • decision record
  • schedule impact
  • reviewed layout
  • Ceiling restoration
  • ductwork
  • change proposal
  • access provision
  • cost impact
  • design preference
  1. Priya
    Daniel, the supply grille looks awkward beside the pendant light. I'd like it moved. Is the small distance on the ceiling a small change to the work?
  2. Daniel
    It is a 1 we can record, but the visible size of the move does not tell us the amount of work involved above the ceiling.
  3. Priya
    Yes, it's an appearance issue. I'm not reporting poor cooling or saying the grille has failed; I want the light and grille to sit better together.
  4. Daniel
    Understood. We should call it a 2, not an established performance defect. That keeps the reason for the request accurate when it reaches the design team.
  5. Priya
    What would need assessment beyond marking a new position on the ceiling plan? I hadn't thought about the connection above it.
  6. Daniel
    The 3 has not been assessed, nor have ceiling restoration, service access, price, or timing. A revised drawing must be supported by review of those related effects.
  7. Priya
    Would restoring the old ceiling opening be part of that review too? I don't want a price that leaves the finishing work unexplained.
  8. Daniel
    Yes. 4 must be assessed rather than treated as automatically included or unnecessary. We should not give you a price for only part of the actual change.
  9. Priya
    Can we also check service access? Moving something to a neater position wouldn't help if it makes the relevant services hard to reach.
  10. Daniel
    Correct. The 5 needs review with the other requirements. Appearance is important, but it does not by itself establish whether a revised arrangement is technically acceptable.
  11. Priya
    What decision can I make now? I don't have the information to design the duct route or choose a technically acceptable new position myself.
  12. Daniel
    You can retain the current 6, or request a coordinated change proposal. Those are the two options at this stage; neither requires you to specify a duct route.
  13. Priya
    I'd like a proposal. Please don't treat that request as acceptance of an unknown extra cost or as permission to start the move.
  14. Daniel
    It does not. A 7 will define the alternative and its consequences for review. Requesting it is separate from accepting the resulting work and commercial terms.
  15. Priya
    Can you keep the original finish date? The office move-in depends on it, though I realize the schedule effect hasn't been assessed yet.
  16. Daniel
    I cannot confirm that yet. The 8 is one of the open questions, along with cost. Your timing concern should be recorded without being mistaken for a promise that no effect exists.
  17. Priya
    Then please record timing as a concern, not as your guarantee. And avoid calling the move free or minor before the full scope is known.
  18. Daniel
    Agreed. The 9 will need a defined scope. We will include ductwork, access, restoration, and timing in the coordinated review rather than price only the visible grille.
  19. Priya
    That's my decision: assess a coordinated proposal and return the consequences for review. I'm not instructing the team to relocate the grille now.
  20. Daniel
    That is the 10: coordinated proposal requested; relocation not authorized. The current reviewed arrangement is not replaced by an assumed new position during this conversation.

Answers and explanations
  1. design preference Turn 2. Design preference identifies the appearance concern without claiming a performance fault.
  2. visual conflict Turn 4. Visual conflict describes the relationship in appearance between the grille and pendant light.
  3. ductwork Turn 6. Ductwork is one of the hidden systems whose changes have not been assessed for the proposed move.
  4. Ceiling restoration Turn 8. Ceiling restoration concerns reinstating affected finishes and remains part of the unassessed change scope.
  5. access provision Turn 10. Access provision concerns how the relevant services remain reachable after a proposed layout change.
  6. reviewed layout Turn 12. Reviewed layout is the existing arrangement available as the first stated option.
  7. change proposal Turn 14. Change proposal is the requested assessed alternative, not authorization to install or acceptance of its terms.
  8. schedule impact Turn 16. Schedule impact remains unassessed, so unchanged completion cannot be guaranteed.
  9. cost impact Turn 18. Cost impact must be assessed with the defined work rather than assumed absent because the visible move seems small.
  10. decision record Turn 20. Decision record preserves the selected assessment step without turning it into an instruction to proceed.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Priya: Daniel, the supply grille looks awkward beside the pendant light. I'd like it moved. Is the small distance on the ceiling a small change to the work?
  2. Daniel: It is a design preference we can record, but the visible size of the move does not tell us the amount of work involved above the ceiling.
  3. Priya: Yes, it's an appearance issue. I'm not reporting poor cooling or saying the grille has failed; I want the light and grille to sit better together.

Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The grille is only the visible part

A technician discusses a proposed ceiling change with the office client.

  1. Technician: You want the supply grille moved because it visually conflicts with the pendant light.
  2. Office client: Yes. It looks like a small cover, so I hoped the change would be simple.
  3. Technician: The connected ductwork and ceiling finishes may be affected; the extent hasn't been assessed.
  4. Office client: Could we obtain a coordinated change proposal rather than alter the agreed layout immediately?
  5. Technician: Yes. Please provide the revised lighting position so both teams review the same arrangement.
  6. Office client: I'd like the current layout kept as an option if the change is disruptive.
  7. Technician: We'll compare the proposed scope, restoration responsibility, price, and timing against that retained option.
  8. Office client: Please include any service-access implications rather than leave those until the ceiling is finished.
  9. Technician: I'll ask the design team to address them explicitly, without prescribing a new route myself.
  10. Office client: Then proceed with the review request, not the physical relocation or an assumed extra order.
The new cabinet occupies service space

A technician and facilities planner review furniture near equipment.

  1. Technician: The proposed storage cabinet overlaps the service-clearance area shown in the equipment detail.
  2. Facilities planner: It's a shallow cabinet. Could we describe it as outside the equipment footprint?
  3. Technician: The footprint and service clearance are different; the access requirement extends beyond the unit itself.
  4. Facilities planner: I see. We need to compare the actual cabinet dimensions with the referenced clearance area.
  5. Technician: Yes, and ask the designer to review the relationship using the correct equipment documentation.
  6. Facilities planner: Would making the cabinet movable automatically resolve the service-access question for this room?
  7. Technician: Not automatically. The revised arrangement still needs review rather than a different label.
  8. Facilities planner: I'll request an alternative storage position and keep the current proposal unapproved.
  9. Technician: Please show the service area on the furniture layout so the reason is visible.
  10. Facilities planner: That will make the coordination issue clearer than telling the client their cabinet simply doesn't fit.
The ceiling contractor's scope is separate

A technician clarifies a relocation estimate with a project coordinator.

  1. Technician: The mechanical estimate covers the proposed ductwork change, but excludes reinstating the ceiling finish.
  2. Project coordinator: The client thought one relocation price included all work associated with moving the opening.
  3. Technician: Then the comparison needs a separate ceiling-restoration item or a clearly coordinated combined proposal.
  4. Project coordinator: I'll ask the ceiling contractor for the same affected area and finish reference.
  5. Technician: Please keep access dates aligned so neither trade assumes the other has already completed preparation.
  6. Project coordinator: The client also wants the pendant moved. That belongs with the electrical team, not this estimate.
  7. Technician: Exactly. We should identify it as another coordinated item rather than hide it under relocation.
  8. Project coordinator: I'll present the inclusions and exclusions plainly, with each responsible team named.
  9. Technician: That lets the client assess the whole requested change without inferring a complete package from one price.
  10. Project coordinator: I'll keep the original reviewed layout available until the revised package is actually accepted.

Two scenarios to practice

Moving a grille is a coordinated change
  1. Read turns 1-10. Name the visual issue and each unassessed effect: ductwork, restoration, access, cost, and timing.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress retain the layout or request a proposal, with no approval to relocate.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Offer two clear paths

Complete the choice discussion. Keep the existing layout and the request for assessment separate from installation approval.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "You can retain the reviewed ___."

Answer and explanation

D. layout

Layout refers to the existing reviewed arrangement available as the first option.

2. Client: "Or request a coordinated change ___."

Answer and explanation

A. proposal

Proposal is the assessed alternative the client may request before deciding.

3. Coordinator: "Cost and timing still need ___."

Answer and explanation

B. assessment

The effects on price and schedule have not yet been evaluated.

4. Client: "This request does not authorize ___."

Answer and explanation

D. relocation

Requesting a proposal does not approve moving the supply grille.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 07: Coordinating ductwork, access, and changes
Original fictional case: Office client Priya asks project coordinator Daniel to move a supply grille because it visually conflicts with a pendant light. The proposal has not assessed ductwork, ceiling restoration, service access, price, or timing. Priya may retain the current reviewed layout or request a coordinated change proposal. Daniel must explain the choice without treating a small visible move as a minor technical task or guaranteeing unchanged cost and completion.
Language workshop: Moving a grille is a coordinated change
Communication goal: Acknowledge an appearance preference, explain unassessed consequences, and present two bounded choices without promising price or timing.
Language guidance: The visible conflict concerns the grille and pendant light. No performance fault is established. Ductwork, restoration, access, price, and timing all need assessment before the proposed move can be treated as a defined change.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand why you want the grille clear of the pendant light. / You are asking to relocate the supply grille. / The visible move may involve work above the ceiling. / Ductwork, restoration, access, price, and timing still need assessment. / The concern is visual, not a confirmed performance defect. / You can retain the current reviewed layout. / You can request a coordinated change proposal. / Those are the two decisions available at this stage. / I cannot confirm unchanged cost before review. / The schedule effect has not been established. / The proposal must account for service access. / Asking for a proposal does not authorize the relocation. / The new position needs the relevant coordination. / Ceiling restoration must be included in the assessment. / You are requesting assessment, not approving installation. / We will bring back a defined proposal with its consequences.
Vocabulary: supply grille relocation: Proposed change to the position of a supply-air grille. / pendant light: Light fitting suspended from the ceiling. / visual conflict: Appearance issue between elements in the same view. / reflected ceiling plan: Drawing of ceiling elements represented as viewed in reflection from below. / ceiling void: Space above the visible ceiling and below the structure above. / branch duct: Duct section branching from a larger distribution route. / duct connection: Interface joining a duct to another component. / flexible duct: Air-conveying duct with a flexible construction. / rigid duct: Air-conveying duct with a relatively fixed form. / service clearance: Space required for relevant maintenance or access. / ceiling restoration: Reinstatement of ceiling finishes after defined work. / access provision: Means included in the design for reaching services. / reviewed layout: Arrangement that has undergone the stated project review. / change proposal: Defined suggestion for altering the existing arrangement. / coordination review: Evaluation of the change across affected elements and trades. / scope impact: Effect of a change on the work required. / cost impact: Effect of a change on price or expenditure. / schedule impact: Effect of a change on timing. / design preference: Client choice concerning the desired arrangement or appearance. / technical feasibility: Whether the proposed arrangement can satisfy relevant technical conditions. / revised layout: Updated arrangement proposed or accepted after a change. / unassessed consequence: Effect not yet evaluated. / instruction to proceed: Direction authorizing agreed work to begin. / decision record: Record of the option selected and its conditions.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Name the visual issue and each unassessed effect: ductwork, restoration, access, cost, and timing. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress retain the layout or request a proposal, with no approval to relocate. Complete and check the choice exchange. Keep a request for assessment separate from agreed work and an unchanged finish date.
Partner: Priya: Office client / Daniel: Project coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the choice discussion. Keep the existing layout and the request for assessment separate from installation approval.

Priya: That's my decision: assess a coordinated proposal and return the consequences for review. I'm not instructing the team to relocate the grille now.
Daniel: That is the decision record: coordinated proposal requested; relocation not authorized. The current reviewed arrangement is not replaced by an assumed new position during this conversation.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Vocabulary: supply grille relocation: Proposed change to the position of a supply-air grille. / pendant light: Light fitting suspended from the ceiling. / visual conflict: Appearance issue between elements in the same view. / reflected ceiling plan: Drawing of ceiling elements represented as viewed in reflection from below. / ceiling void: Space above the visible ceiling and below the structure above. / branch duct: Duct section branching from a larger distribution route. / duct connection: Interface joining a duct to another component. / flexible duct: Air-conveying duct with a flexible construction. / rigid duct: Air-conveying duct with a relatively fixed form. / service clearance: Space required for relevant maintenance or access. / ceiling restoration: Reinstatement of ceiling finishes after defined work. / access provision: Means included in the design for reaching services. / reviewed layout: Arrangement that has undergone the stated project review. / change proposal: Defined suggestion for altering the existing arrangement. / coordination review: Evaluation of the change across affected elements and trades. / scope impact: Effect of a change on the work required. / cost impact: Effect of a change on price or expenditure. / schedule impact: Effect of a change on timing. / design preference: Client choice concerning the desired arrangement or appearance. / technical feasibility: Whether the proposed arrangement can satisfy relevant technical conditions. / revised layout: Updated arrangement proposed or accepted after a change. / unassessed consequence: Effect not yet evaluated. / instruction to proceed: Direction authorizing agreed work to begin. / decision record: Record of the option selected and its conditions.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Name the visual issue and each unassessed effect: ductwork, restoration, access, cost, and timing. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress retain the layout or request a proposal, with no approval to relocate. Complete and check the choice exchange. Keep a request for assessment separate from agreed work and an unchanged finish date.
Partner: Priya: Office client / Daniel: Project coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the choice discussion. Keep the existing layout and the request for assessment separate from installation approval.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Priya: Then please record timing as a concern, not as your guarantee. And avoid calling the move free or minor before the full scope is known.
Daniel: Agreed. The cost impact will need a defined scope. We will include ductwork, access, restoration, and timing in the coordinated review rather than price only the visible grille.
Priya: That's my decision: assess a coordinated proposal and return the consequences for review. I'm not instructing the team to relocate the grille now.
Daniel: That is the decision record: coordinated proposal requested; relocation not authorized. The current reviewed arrangement is not replaced by an assumed new position during this conversation.
END REFERENCE
08Handing over commissioning and maintenance informationReport received, seasonal check still open+

Your goal

Separate document receipt from completed verification and distinguish assigned correction work from an unassigned scheduling responsibility.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Report received, seasonal check still open

Client Ben receives a commissioning report for a fictional office system. It lists a deferred seasonal check, and the maintenance sheet names the wrong model. Coordinator Nia accepts responsibility for document correction and will update Ben on Friday. The seasonal-check date is not booked, and responsibility for scheduling it has not been confirmed. The handover must preserve that ownership gap. No overall performance conclusion, completed seasonal result, or change to warranty terms is supplied.

Ben: Client / Nia: Coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the commissioning report list?

Answer and explanation

D. A deferred seasonal check

The report explicitly identifies a check that has been deferred rather than completed.

2. What responsibility does Nia accept?

Answer and explanation

B. Document correction and a Friday update

Nia's commitment is to document correction and communication, not the unassigned scheduling task.

3. What remains unresolved about the seasonal check?

Answer and explanation

C. Both the date and the scheduling owner

The check is unbooked and responsibility for arranging it is not confirmed.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The report has been received, but the seasonal check is deferred. The wrong-model maintenance sheet needs correction. Neither delivery nor a corrected document would itself establish a completed seasonal result or overall system performance.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
commissioning
Process of verifying and documenting system performance against relevant project requirements.distinguish commissioning from document delivery
commissioning report
Record of commissioning activities, results, and outstanding matters.receive the commissioning report
deferred check
Verification postponed until a later appropriate time or condition.record the deferred check
seasonal check
Verification associated with relevant seasonal operating conditions.schedule the seasonal check
maintenance sheet
Document describing relevant maintenance information for equipment.correct the maintenance sheet
model-specific guidance
Information applicable to the actual identified product model.obtain model-specific guidance
document mismatch
Difference between supplied information and the relevant equipment.identify the document mismatch
correction owner
Person responsible for resolving a record error.name the correction owner
scheduling owner
Person responsible for arranging the required attendance or check.confirm the scheduling owner
ownership gap
Task for which responsibility has not been confirmed.preserve the ownership gap
unbooked check
Required verification with no confirmed appointment.retain the unbooked check
update commitment
Promise to provide further information at a stated time.honor the update commitment
outstanding verification
Check not yet completed or accepted.track outstanding verification
handover register
Record of delivered information and remaining handover matters.update the handover register
operating record
Documented information about actual operation.retain the operating record
performance result
Established outcome from relevant measurement or verification.distinguish a performance result
acceptance criterion
Requirement used to judge whether an outcome meets the agreed standard.identify the acceptance criterion
test condition
Defined state under which a verification is carried out.record the test condition
deferral reason
Explanation for postponing an activity.preserve the deferral reason
closeout status
Current position of remaining completion matters.state the closeout status
maintenance requirement
Relevant action or condition specified for upkeep.verify the maintenance requirement
warranty terms
Actual conditions and coverage stated in the relevant warranty.preserve warranty terms
document receipt
Confirmation that information has been delivered.acknowledge document receipt
responsibility readback
Repetition of assigned and unassigned tasks for clarity.give a responsibility readback

Nia owns document correction and promises a Friday update. The seasonal check has no booked date and no confirmed scheduling owner. Preserve both gaps instead of silently assigning the check to Nia or treating Friday as its appointment.

C Notice the language

Do not let one named owner hide a different unassigned task

A coordinator can own a document correction without owning every remaining technical action. A useful handover names the received records, the unresolved checks, and who is responsible for each. State an ownership gap directly rather than letting a polite promise to update imply it is settled.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the report
You have received the commissioning report.
Name the exception
The report lists a deferred seasonal check.
Identify the document error
The maintenance sheet names the wrong model.
Accept a specific task
I will take responsibility for document correction.
Commit to an update
I will update you on Friday.
Keep the date limited
Friday is an update date, not a booked seasonal check.
State the booking gap
The seasonal-check date is not booked.
State the ownership gap
Responsibility for arranging that check is not confirmed.
Avoid implied ownership
My document task does not settle who schedules the check.
Preserve model specificity
The replacement sheet must match the actual model.
Separate receipt and verification
Receiving the report does not complete the deferred check.
Avoid an overall assurance
We have no overall performance conclusion to add here.
Keep warranty separate
This discussion does not change the actual warranty terms.
Name the next coordination issue
We still need a confirmed scheduling owner and date.
Read back responsibilities
Nia owns correction; seasonal scheduling ownership remains open.
Close with accurate status
Documents are received, corrections and deferred verification remain visible.
6 language and precision notes
Lists a deferred check
Describes a recorded exception rather than a completed result.
Will update
Commits to communication without guaranteeing an unresolved outcome.
Responsibility not confirmed
Makes an ownership gap explicit.
Does not settle
Prevents one accepted task from silently resolving another.
Must match
Connects the maintenance document to the relevant equipment model.
Remain visible
Keeps unfinished items in the handover instead of hiding them in a general completion claim.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which summary preserves all responsibilities?

Answer and explanation

B. Nia corrects documents and updates Friday; seasonal-check owner and date remain open

The summary keeps Nia's accepted task distinct from the unresolved scheduling responsibility.

2. What does Friday identify?

Answer and explanation

B. Nia's promised update

The only Friday commitment is communication about follow-up.

3. Which inference is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. Correcting the maintenance sheet completes the seasonal check

A document correction is not performance of the deferred verification.

4. What should the handover keep open?

Answer and explanation

C. Correct model guidance, deferred verification, scheduling owner, and date

The remaining items are separate; receipt, a document task, and an update commitment do not complete verification, assign scheduling ownership, or book a check.

E Conversations

Report received, seasonal check still open

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Client / Nia: Coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • document mismatch
  • ownership gap
  • deferred check
  • performance result
  • scheduling owner
  • handover register
  • responsibility readback
  • warranty terms
  • model-specific guidance
  • update commitment
  1. Ben
    Nia, the commissioning report arrived, but it lists a deferred seasonal check. The maintenance sheet also names a different model from our actual system.
  2. Nia
    Those are two distinct handover matters. I accept the 1 on the maintenance sheet and will take responsibility for correcting that information.
  3. Ben
    Thanks for taking the document correction. I want to separate that from the outstanding check rather than mark everything complete on receipt of the report.
  4. Nia
    No. The 2 is still outstanding. The report records its status; delivery of that report does not carry out the verification or supply its result.
  5. Ben
    Who is arranging the seasonal check? I also can't find a booked date, so both responsibility and scheduling seem unresolved.
  6. Nia
    The 3 has not been confirmed, and no date is booked. I should not imply that my document-correction task resolves either of those questions.
  7. Ben
    I nearly assigned that to you because you're handling the wrong sheet. That would add a responsibility you haven't actually accepted.
  8. Nia
    Exactly. We need to preserve the 4 for the seasonal check. It needs an explicit assignment, not an assumption based on who is speaking at handover.
  9. Ben
    What date can I put down for the next update? I need a communication commitment even while the technical appointment remains unbooked.
  10. Nia
    I will correct the document information and make an 5 for Friday. That gives you a communication date without presenting an unbooked seasonal check as scheduled.
  11. Ben
    Please match the corrected sheet to the actual model. A similar product leaflet would still leave the maintenance information uncertain.
  12. Nia
    Agreed. We need 6 for the actual equipment. Correcting the sheet is a defined records task; it is not evidence that the seasonal verification has occurred.
  13. Ben
    Would report received support a statement that the system has passed every operating condition? The deferred check suggests that would be too broad.
  14. Nia
    No. We have no overall 7 to add from these facts. The deferred item remains part of the record, and we should not invent a successful result for it.
  15. Ben
    I also want the summary to preserve the actual warranty terms, without implying the open check creates a new warranty promise.
  16. Nia
    Correct. The 8 are not changed by this conversation. Neither an update date nor an outstanding check creates a new warranty promise here.
  17. Ben
    Let me check the responsibilities: you own document correction and the Friday update. The seasonal-check owner and date are both still unconfirmed.
  18. Nia
    That 9 is accurate. It makes the commitment and both remaining scheduling questions visible instead of assigning the whole handover to one person by implication.
  19. Ben
    I'll acknowledge receipt and keep the unresolved items separate. Friday goes in the update column, not the seasonal-test appointment column.
  20. Nia
    Thank you. The 10 should preserve the report receipt, wrong-model correction, deferred check, and open scheduling responsibility and date. That is a clear close to today's conversation, with the unfinished work still visible.

Answers and explanations
  1. document mismatch Turn 2. Document mismatch identifies the wrong-model maintenance sheet as a correction task.
  2. deferred check Turn 4. Deferred check remains outstanding even though its existence is recorded in the delivered report.
  3. scheduling owner Turn 6. Scheduling owner has not been confirmed, so the arranging responsibility remains open.
  4. ownership gap Turn 8. Ownership gap names the unassigned scheduling responsibility rather than silently giving it to Nia.
  5. update commitment Turn 10. Update commitment is Nia's promise to communicate on Friday, not a promised technical-check result.
  6. model-specific guidance Turn 12. Model-specific guidance links the corrected maintenance information to the actual equipment rather than a similar model.
  7. performance result Turn 14. Performance result cannot be generalized to all conditions when a seasonal check is still deferred.
  8. warranty terms Turn 16. Warranty terms retain their actual meaning and are not revised by the document update or deferred-check discussion.
  9. responsibility readback Turn 18. Responsibility readback distinguishes the assigned document task from the unassigned seasonal scheduling task.
  10. handover register Turn 20. Handover register should record receipt alongside corrections and outstanding verification without claiming full completion.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: Nia, the commissioning report arrived, but it lists a deferred seasonal check. The maintenance sheet also names a different model from our actual system.
  2. Nia: Those are two distinct handover matters. I accept the document mismatch on the maintenance sheet and will take responsibility for correcting that information.
  3. Ben: Thanks for taking the document correction. I want to separate that from the outstanding check rather than mark everything complete on receipt of the report.
  4. Nia: No. The deferred check is still outstanding. The report records its status; delivery of that report does not carry out the verification or supply its result.
  5. Ben: Who is arranging the seasonal check? I also can't find a booked date, so both responsibility and scheduling seem unresolved.
  6. Nia: The scheduling owner has not been confirmed, and no date is booked. I should not imply that my document-correction task resolves either of those questions.
  7. Ben: I nearly assigned that to you because you're handling the wrong sheet. That would add a responsibility you haven't actually accepted.
  8. Nia: Exactly. We need to preserve the ownership gap for the seasonal check. It needs an explicit assignment, not an assumption based on who is speaking at handover.
  9. Ben: What date can I put down for the next update? I need a communication commitment even while the technical appointment remains unbooked.
  10. Nia: I will correct the document information and make an update commitment for Friday. That gives you a communication date without presenting an unbooked seasonal check as scheduled.
  11. Ben: Please match the corrected sheet to the actual model. A similar product leaflet would still leave the maintenance information uncertain.
  12. Nia: Agreed. We need model-specific guidance for the actual equipment. Correcting the sheet is a defined records task; it is not evidence that the seasonal verification has occurred.
  13. Ben: Would report received support a statement that the system has passed every operating condition? The deferred check suggests that would be too broad.
  14. Nia: No. We have no overall performance result to add from these facts. The deferred item remains part of the record, and we should not invent a successful result for it.
  15. Ben: I also want the summary to preserve the actual warranty terms, without implying the open check creates a new warranty promise.
